WOMEN'S CHAT.

----- The Baroness Beaumont.—The- little Baroness I Beaumont who has just celebrated another anniversary of her birthday, is ten years old, and one of the four ladien entitled to call themselves by this title. It will be remeinbered that Queen Victoria had the title called out of abeyance in this little maiden's favour, eight years7ago, after the death of her father, which was due 1 to a gun accident. The Baroness has one sister to whom si.e is devotedly attached. Only Daughters.—The marriage of the only daughter I of the house is always regarded with mixed feelings by the bride's parents, and Lady Aberdeen, while rejoicin,g in her daughter's happiness is sadly missing her con- stunt and cheerful companionship. The two ladies were devoted to good works. soma of which they must now carry on separately, and were never so happy as when busy with their charitable engalements. Captain and Lady Marjory Sinclair have been spending the first part of their honeymoon at Gwydyr Castle, one of the most ancient castles in these. Isles of ours. Lord and Lady Carrington have frequently entertained Royalty at this, their beautiful and historical seat. The Queen and the Fashions.—That it is possible to be beautifully and becomingly dressed without living in a perpetual rush after the very latest fashions, is a truth that has been brought home to us times ont of number by her Majesty the Queen. For example,though almost everyone is now wearing the huge sleeve, her Majesty remains faithful to the tightly fitting variety, and in the matter of skirts the Queen still elects to have them built closely fitting about the hips,no gathers or fullness of any kind appearing about the waist of any of her toilettes. If those smart "Society women who insist upon following closely all the freaks of fashion would imitate the Queen's example, and wear only what suits them, regardless of the whims and fancies of the modistes, they would present a more artistic appearance than is the case with very many of the seekers after novelties and eccentricities in the modistic world. Cotton Tussore.—A quite new and Very delightful fabric is cotton tussore. Like the old familiar silken variety, it can be had in all the newest shades as well as in the old-world, ecru tint, and unlike the majority of linens it crushes very little, and promises to wear excellently. Red, brown, and orange are very favourite colours at the moment, and soon we shall be entering upon all the new autumn tints, many of which are novel, and likely to prove most becoming. Modes for Golfing.--Red is in high favour for golfing the short red skirt and sailor hat being donned with a white, cream or ecru tinted blouse. A rod underskirt, soft red leather belt, very wide, and fastening in front with a large red buckle, and a loose dark red cape. com- pletes a very striking and effective toilette. Great care needs to be exeicised however, in the selection of the tone of red, some complexions needing a bright, and others a dull shade of this particular colour. When the complexion permits, the former should be chosen, it being the more modish and desirable in every way. New Millinery.—Divers new shapes are appearing in the millinery world, and nowadays nothing, however eccentric attracts attention. One can make or bend a shape as one will, and so long as it is becoming that is all one need consider. The mushroom variety is having a great vogue again, and also is our old friend the tall crowned Empire hat. The latter is always becom- ing provided it is poised rightly, and the hair is dressed correctly to take it, but alas, so many women fail to select just the right style of hairdressing, or the right angle, and then the hat is unjustly held responsible for the undesirable effect. One point to remember in the selection of dressy hats is the effectiveness of the semi- transparent brim. rhe colours of fruit, flowers or feathers placed above, glint through it, and thereby enhance the soft tones of the complexion. This is a far more becoming idea than that of arranging flowers beneath the brim, and resting on the hair. The Passing of the Ponch.—Many of the newest frocks are being built with tightly-fitting waists coming to a long stiffly-boned point in front. To the taffetas gowns. which we are holding in such high esteem just now, the mode is eminently suited, but unfortunately it needs a very perfect figure, which very few of us possess. Still we have pouched and flopped so long that reaction was bound to come, and there is very little doubt that the day of the" pouch" is over. But there are always compensations, and if we lose the pouch we may gain a pocket. A fashionable woman was heard to remark the other day that she had taken all her luggage for a week-end visit in her two sleeves and "pouch," and though this may rather over-estimate the accommo- dation provided in this way, we shall sorely miss the comforts of the pouch. The tailors, as if to make ud for the hard-heartedness of the fashionable dress- makers, are giving us quite a number of pockets in our new autumn coats, but then coats are only possible for outdoor wear. Tenderness of the Feet.—Dnring the holidays many people, especially those who walk little at any other season of the year, suffer front tenderness of the feet. To prevent this, they should be bathed twice daily—in the morning and at night—in lukewarm water, to which a little vinegar has been added. After drying the feet they should be dusted over with a mixture of boracic, and starch powder—two ounces of the starch to one of the boracic. Only easy. comfortable shoes should be worn at holiday times, and when purchasing footgear, it is well to give the best price our purse will permit us to go to. Cheap boots and shoes are even worse than cheap gloves; they never look well, and are seldom .really comfortable. - Second-hand Motors.—The number of second-hand motor cars being offered just now is amazing—so also is the tremendous reduction which is being made in the -price of them. No doubt many geeuine bargains are to be had, but in the generality of cases, there is some serious defect which renders the car dear at any price. Even though it may bear the name of one of the best makers, that is no guarantee that it is not a failure. The best firms, in the course of experiments occasionally turn out failures, and these are soon offered at a price that is reduced beyonrones wildest dreams. Only an expert should be trusted to select a second-hand motor, and even so it is hotter to get the makers to overhaul the car, before completing the purchase. Dirty Railway Trains.—The " bogey " just now seems I

to be the dangers arising from dirty trains. Numbers of people have been writing to the papers and advocat- ing the return to the wooden seats and backs of the third class "cattle truck" description, but probably the majority of us would prefer to run the risks said to be incurred by cushions, and the.. stuffs which cover them. If, however, the vast amount of talking and discussion in the papers which is going on at the moment, leads to the stirring up of the railway companies to devise some plan of occasionally disinfecting their "stuffed carriages" it will certainly have attained some useful purpose.

MADGE.

GOOD TEMPLARY.

Hall-yearly Convention at Warrington. The half-yearly session of the No. 1 Convention, South-West Lanzashire, of the 1.0.G.T, was held in Ebenezer Church,Buttermarket street,on Sattirday. The session was opened by Bro. J. Oliver, District Chaplain, and representatives were present from Warrington, 141arlestown, Wigan, Hindlcy, Widnes, Leigh and St. Helens and a military brother was present from Bloem- fontein.

Bro. E. Newman C. Sec., (Earlestown), in his quar- terly report stated that the work during the past half year had not been as good as he would have liked it to be. There had been six deaths during the half-year, in- cluding Sister I3attersby P.C.V.T. and Bro. John darke of Earlestown who were both being buried that day. The statistical returns showed a decrease in membership. Some lodges were not meeting owing to the difficulty of getting members together during the summer months. He urged the members to rally and try and make the convention a success. In the report of the Convention Treasurer (Bro. J. Warburton) showed a balance in hand of £3 17s. 7d. Brother F. Merlin. C.E.S. (Warring- ton) in the electoral report complained of the lack of interest in this department and alluded to the Licensing Bill and to the stand made by Mr. Asquith, Mr. Lloyd George, Sir H. Campbell-Bannerman, Earl Spencer, and the Archbishop of Canterbury on the bill. Bro. J. Parr, C.C.T. (Leigh), in his report made a feeling reference to the many deaths in the Convention area. He urged the members not to be discouraged but work on in the fight. Sister Mrs. Woods, C.S.J.T. (Earlestown), reported a decrease in the juvenile branch, the only increase being in the "Path of Safety" Leigh. The reports were adopted on the motion of Bro. Punshon (St. Helens), seconded by Bro.-J. Sheath (Warrington.) Bro. Oliver gave a brief address OR the work of the Order and thanked the Convention for their reception of him that afternoon. Votes of condolence were orderedto be sent to Bro. G. Clarke (Earlestown), Sister Makin (St. Helens); Sister Lowe (Wigan), and the relatives of the late Sister Battersby and Bro. Batchelor (Leigh.) The election of officers was then proceeded with and resulted as follows :—C.C.T., Bro. .1. Parr (Leigh); C.Co., Bro. F. Merlin (Warrington) ; C.E:S., Bro. C. J. Sheath (Warrington) C.S.J.T. Sister Mrs. Woods (Earlestown) ; C.T. Bro. J. War- burton (Earlestown) ; C. Chaplain, Bro. P. Hulme (Leigh) ; C. Secretary', Bro. C. Newman (Earlestown); C.V.T., Sister A. Littlewood (Warrington) ; C. IVlar.. Bro. W. Rose (Earlestown) ; C.D.M., Sister E. Clarke (Widnes) ; C.X.S., Bro. Newcombe (Wigan); C. Guard, Bro. Morris (Leigh); C. Sentinel, Bro. Lewis (Widnes) ; C.P.C.T., Bro. Blundell (Hindley). The new offlcers were installed by Bro. Oliver, D. Ch. A well-attended open-air meeting was held after tea in conjunction with the Salvation Army, when addresses were delivered by several members of both organiza- tions.

The Examiner Saturday, August 20, 1904.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

The continuance of the hot weather ha,,, made the thinnest of muslin gowns absolutely necessary to one's . comfort for the heat has been quite tropiial. Fortunately for all lovers of pretty things, fashion had approved of the most diaphanous materials for this season's wear and there is, therefore, no lack of pretty patterns and exquisite colours to choose from and the prices are as reasonable as the materials are lovely, placing them within the reach of everyone. The sales proved a gold mine to those who had delayed their purchases and there was during the last two or three weeks quite a scramble for the cool 'linen and muslin frocks. The be which boasts a skirt already tucked and lace inserted ready for drawing into a waistband has been much sought after for the material for the bodice which is sold with the skirt can readily be run up into one or other of the simple, yet pretty, blouse bodices, for which paper patterns can be obtained cut to one's

===== 0002601_19040820_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
PRETTY TOILETTE.

measure. The girl who has a more or less extensive knowledge of practical dressmaking can, by investing in one or two of these robes, keep herself smartly gowned in an up-to-date style at a really small expense. One under- dress can be used for more than one toilette if desired, in this way reducing not only the cost of the gowns but the quantity of luggage one is forced to take from home. The gowns of to-day are so fussy that they take up a great deal of room and require careful packing with plenty of tissue paper in between the folds to ensure them arriving in good order.

A pretty toilette is given in my -first sketch and is, made of thin white chinese washable silk made in a smart style free from any exaggeration. The bodice has a deep yoke of lace finished by a ruche of lace while another ruche of the same kind completes the full frill which falls over the shoulders. The sleeves are very full and are drawn into a band of lace extending down the inner seam of the sleeve to the deep cuff, which it joins. The simple skirt has two full frills headed by gaugings and completed by the lace ruche. Nothing could be prettier or cooler than a frock of this description for a summer after- noon. Some people have found even the transparent lace collar band too warm, and we have the lace yoke cut in a slight V back and front finished by an inter - threaded narrow satin ribbr T le who are posses-

AN EVENING BLOUSE.

sed of plump, well-rounded throats will nnd this a most becoming style, but the lace collar should otherwise be selected as it conceals many deficiencies. There are some very pretty embroidered linen dresses made in a simple style, white worked with pale brown thread being most approved and chic. These frocks wash nicely and always look fresh, but as they cannot be worn with success more than twice in consequence of crushing easily they are only for the girl who has a very varied wardrobe. The charming blouse, depicted is for 'gummer evening wear, although it could be transformed into a mode suited for the afternoon by the addition of a lace yoke. It is made of soft pink crepe de chine which is arranged fichu wise round' the shoulders, a trimming of smart little pink satin bows decorating the front. The sleeves fall in a full pouf just to below the elbows where a deep frill of lace falls to the Wrists. The bodice pouches fully over a swathed band of satin ribbon. Crepe de chine is a material which has rendered itself beloved by reason of its artistic possibilities as there is no material I fancy which falls in softer folds and can be draped to perfection. In white it is washable with a little care and is therefore to be selected even before the chiffon blouse, which, though absolutely perfect for summer wear (especial" kola pleated)

A PRETTY HAT.

does not stand much wear as it soon loses its looks. There are one or two other materials which imitate chiffon wonderfully closely, but they in no case quite come up to the original fabric for softness and beauty. The pretty hat depicted is of fine tuscan straw trimmed with large cabbage roses and a band of pale blue satin ribbon round the crown. A lace scarf forms a fall at the back and a band which is passed under the chin and secured at the other side of the brim in the picturesque fashion which has so caught on this year. Sometimes lace is used for these ties or bands while others again em- ployed wide but very soft satin ribbon which is tied in a bow and the ends allowed to stream over the shoulders. The mushroom hat has become more fashionable of late as it shades the face so nicely. It is improved in every way from what it was half a century ago, and is now rendered .more becoming by a bandeau which raises it , and does' away with the flattened dowdy look which it originally possessed. In those days (so it looks to us) people did not consider the point as to whether or not their clothes were becoming but rested content with, the com- forting thought that they were costly. A, small close silk ruche forms the usual trim- ming to a 'mushroom hat although sometimes a wide bow is placed flat on the brim in front or a stiff wreath of tiny roses arranged round the crown.

FALL OF THE PHARISEE,

BY CLEMENTINA BLACK,

Author oi "The PrinC:ess Desiree,' 'Miss Falkland,"

An Ap;itato4''

&c,

A young man and a young woman were sitting tcgether in an old garden. They were keeping up no sustained conversation, but were each quietly and happily satisfied.in. the -company of the other.. "You know that Leo Stevens is at home again?" the girl said presently. "I know," the Young man answered briefly. "Letty was telling me about him. They hope he really will settle down this time." "T-hey have k,lped that a good many times, haven't they ?" She shot a lock at him, disturbed, uneasy, ques- tioning, almost appealing; but his eyes were on the path at their feet, and the look missed its aim. "I can't understand having much hope of a person like that, he said again, after a minute's uncom- fortable pause. "Can't you?" said the girl gently. It was she now whose eyes were turned away. Presently she added: "He has had a great deal against him." Her voice had the same infusion of appeal that had been just now in her glance. This time it reached him, and lie hardened before it, answering sharply: Of his own making, then. I think he has had everything for him, except his own conduct. I don't want to be setting up for an example, but What is"there in which he didn't start even with me? And why should not he do as well as I have done ? "

"You!" said the girl. Her tone meant clearly "I measure you by another standard," and the self-tormeriting instinct of a man in love caused her hearer to set the difference to his own disadvantage. As for her, like many another woman, she had a clear eye for the nature of any man for 'whom she cared nothing, and a ready fund of coquetry at his service; but in regard to James Southgate she was too much in earnesi, too much personally concerned; for the necessary detachment; nnd, while always considering his character, she was seldom observant of his feelings. The idea that he might feel jealous of Leo Stevens had never entered her mind, but her woman's susceptibility to an atmosphere of disapproval would have kept her frail speaking of him again. Not so James; his very uneasiness' brought him 1-_sck to the topic. "You have hope, then, of Leopold Stevens?" "Certainly. I should be sorry to despair of any- one at his age." . "A man who has never done any good till he is six-and-twenty is not very likely to begin then." "No man is ever likely to do much good who is never believed in." James muttered something about belief depending on facts, not uffon hopes. They sat silent, she thinking : "He is hard, intolerant, unmerciful. Could one bear to live beside a person with so little pity for faults?" ; he thinking that not even such a woman as Emily could resist the- charm of good looks and easy manners. Each a little discontented with the other, they went into the ugly, comfort able house; where Emily's uncle, emerging from behind his newspaper, said: "Ha! James, wing to give me a game of chess?" But James. usually attentive to every wish of Emily's netrest relative, professed to-night that he had no time. The sins of Leo Stevens were in his mind as he walked homewards. At school he remembered him idle, shifty, not unkind or ill- tempered, but perfectly. inconsiderate of anything beyond his own desires, involved always in scrapes, and finally in a strong suspicion of actual dis- honesty. After their school days Leo had gone to London as a medical student, and James—who would have liked to go to London too—stayed in Greysham at his father's brewery. After a year or two, Leo had come home under some shadow of unexplained disgrace. and the young men had been a good deal tllfown together. ,Greysham was little more than a village,, and the few families forming its sociay had long-standing links of amity; Leo's father and James's father, Leo's mother and James's mother, had been on friendly terms for five-and-twenty years; James himself began to feel for Leo some of the liking which comes of habit, and Leo cultivated carefully the liking of James. The return of Emily Westerall, who during these months was away, would, no doubt, have awakened James's self-distrustful jealousy, and ended the truce: but it was ended sooner, and by the act of Leo. They were walking home together one even- ing from Lillingbourne, the county town, of which Greysliam was fast becoming a suburb, when James recollected that he ought to have made a certain payment for his father, and had forgotten it. Said Leo gaily: "Oh, I'm going over early in the morning; I'll pay it for you." James, with many thanks, handed over to his .iompanion three five-pound notes, and explained that rue amount due was twelve pounds and some Going the next evening to Leo for the receipt and the change, he found the latter not forthcoming. "Oh, the fellow hadn't change," said Leo, "so I -aid I'd call for it when I went over to-morrow." James, a little annoyed, found himself Obliged to explain these circumstances to his father, and to :lear himself and Leo and the Lillingbourne trades- man blamed for unbusinesslike conduct. "I ought to have done my own business, and I'll eall for that change myself this afternoon." In the afternoon, during Mr. Southgate 's absence, in came Leo, pale and agitated, to confess that he 'lad had the money and had lost it at billiards. For the moment he was unable to repay it, and he entreated almost with tears that James would keep 'ifs secret and help him out of his difficulty—he was sure he would be able to pay the money in a 2ew days' time. It was for his father's sake, who had done so much for him, that he dreaded ex- posure. James finally promised to keep the story ;ecret, and to provide the missing amount. The father presently returned, full of perplexed and Angry suspicions. The tradesman had declared that the change had been given at once; lie had gone to Leo, and Leo had said that James bad it. Where was it, and what did it all mean? James handed out the money in silence. "When did Stevens give it you!" his father asked, still doubtful; and James unwillingly found himself driven to the equivocal reply: "Not till to-day." From that day he had never willingly exchanged a, word with Leo. For many weeks the shadow of .listrust lingered between himself and his father; and for years it remained disagreeable to him to meet the eye of the tradesman concerned. The money-had never been repaid. For a few weeks Leo told himself that he would pay it to-morrow; for a week more that he would pay it at a convenient opportunity; then he left off thinking about it, and by this time had probably succeeded in forgetting the whole incident. And now the scapegrace was home again, and Emily Westerall was ready to have hopes of him. James walked home ruminating this bitter fact. Emily, meanwhile, whose comprehension of Leo was a great deal fuller than his, was, for all her kindly desires, very little occupied with the problematic future of that young gentleman. To her Leo was important only as a touchstone of James; and it was always on James's account, and never on Leo's, that she regretfully considered and reconsidered that streak of uncharitableness.

It was perhaps an undefined dread of Leo which led James a few days later to speak plainly to Emily lf his own feelings and hopes. They were standing together In Mr. Westerall's garden and watching a glorious sunset. His hand quietly possessed itself of hers, and hers was not withdrawn. "Emily," said he, after a moment, "there is not much need to say it; you know I love you; you have known it a long time, and you haven't drawn hack."

Her hand hung in his quite willingly, but sh said nothing. "May I take it that you will trust me and that we may go on together?" "I—don't know." Emily murmured. "I —can't quite make up my mind." , Of course he demanded explanations, and . she stumblingly exposed her doubts of their being alike enough in their views—"the other day, for instance, when you spoke of Leo Stevens." James heard the name with an angry pang, which in a man quicker of speech might have produced an angry outburst and led to a clear explanation on both sides. As it was, he remained almost silent, went home presently disconsolate, having ben neither accepted nor refused, and left Emily telling herself that they could never make each other happy. By an extra touch of ill-luck, the retreating lover met, almost on the doorstep, Leo Stevens. evidently bound for Mr. Westerall's, adorned with a gay blossom in the front of his coat, and humming a cheerful air. The frown of James deepened. and he seized this excellent opportunity to torment himself by imagining conversations between Emily and his enemy. next afternoon that, being in the old established Westerall, its pro- pcLori eitnitntwoygra.bsosbtouaanrnnnkdte.l be standing vniednroy observed sheer passing d r3v,l, any just about to get into his dog-cart,and was at once invited to driv4 home with him- Presently, when they had exchanged the streets of the town for the sandy laner of the country, Mr. James Westerall began: "You know Leo Stevens ?'" James, of course, assented. "Well, I don't know what to do about him exactly. His father wants me to take him into the bank." "The hank !"

"He hasn't been very steady hitherto—we all know that." James was silent. "I have known his father a_ grteht many year.o

"That's 'what they all say," James thought. "And then, he doesn't seem. such a bad sort di young fellow, and he says he's going to turn over a new leaf—bin of course he say g that. Now, you have known lin a long time and a great deal better than I have. Do you think it would be safe—wise I don't say?" "I don't think well of him, certainly." "No, no; 1 suppose not, poor fellow; but one woulel be sorry to take away a chance of doing better. • And I have known his father t iese twenty years." The idea of having Leo established in Greysham was particularly dislileasing. to James. It was, how- ever, never very easy to him to find words, and the difficulty began at this moment to irritate him. "And yet I should not like to take anyone into She bank who Was not trustwo.thy." These words of doubt threw back James upon kindlier feelings towards Leo. But Mr. Westerall, luite blind to the workings of his companion's inind, fancied that his observations had carriEd more weight than he intended, and hastened to balance them by something on the other side. "Emily seems to wish it very much. It was her Tuggestion. YOu know how fond she is of Letty Stevens." For an instant wrath took possession of the soul of Tames. Leo supported by Emily's advocacy, kept at her side by her own choice! He broke out, in a voice half an octave deeper than his own: "Ilow dare he think of it? Why, he's a liar, and a swindler, and a cheat!" Then, his voice rising again to its own note, and his words pouring out in unwdnted rapidity, he told the tale, briefly, sharply, with clear and biting words. Mr. Westerall's comfortable, easy-going habits of thought were terribly disturbed. "It's—it's very sad," he murmured, and found nothing else to say. When their roads parted James, as he sprang down, asked: "Shall you take him into the bank?" and Mr. West.?rall answered: "I'd as soon take a viper. I'm much obliged to you for telling me." James winced a little at the thanks. An uncomfortable self-reproach began, before long; to stir in the recesses of his conscience, and he held it carefully back. For tw,) or three days he avoided the company of Emily and her uncle; a vague sense of guilt haunted even the thought of them. But at last he found himself again sitting at dinner in the familiar room, and experiencing the familiar sense of peace and ease that for him radiated from Emily. Presently Mr. Westerall said: "By the bye, James, have you heard anything of young Stevens?" "No," said James, and a sharp pang of self- reproach ran through him. He had been off his guard, and the lurking dragon of self-reproach had seized the opportunity to push .its head to the surface.

"You know he's in London?" the host proceeded. "No," answered the guest again. "Oh, yes; Jackson met him the other day. He seems to be going on quite in his old ways again. Actually wanted to borrow half-a-crown of Jackson." He paused and sipped his wine. James sat wait- ing in terror for the next words—and tDey came. "I am very much obliged to you for warning me against him. I should certainly have taken him into the bank, and what a terrible thing that would have been." _

James glanced across at Emily, and met her widoning gaze of pained surprise. He dropped his face. The remembrance of his broken promise came upon him like a flood. The rest of the dinner was a bad dream. Emily rose and went away, and at last the moment came when Mr. Westerall composed himself to slumber, and James was free to go after her. In the doorway of the drawing-room he paused, transfixed. Emily, the calm, the self-controlled, was sitting on a sofa, her face buried in the cushions, her whole person shaken by silent sobs. With the sensations of a criminal, James withdrew and went home to a sleepless fig ht He who had walked so surely, so proudly aware that he could never nourish an unjust thought or do an un- generous action, had fallen to a depth below injustice and ungenerosity ; he had broken his promise, and that out of no desire for mere truth, but for the sake of removing his on enemy. Out of his intense humiliation grew slowTy a perception of how hard a measure he had deaA to those who stumbled. Now he knew that, though he had fallen into a base and hateful action, he was not himself base. There were temptations and impulses of which ha had not been aware, and perhaps they might be stronger for others than 'or him. By degrees it became clear to him that in e ought to go and make confession to Leo; he drfw close this most displeasing thought, like a hair sl art. Next morning, as he walked with his father towards the brewery, he took the nem t step in his penance. - • "Mr. Westerall asked me the other day," he began abruptly, "about Leo Stevens; he was thinkiEg of takinir him into the bank."

"I think he might have known better. What did you say to him?" "I told him what I had promised never to tell anyone—how he cheated me about that change of yours three years ago. And now Mr. WeSterall won't have anything to do with him." "Well, of course not. I suppose that is what you expected." "I don't know what I expected. I was angry, and I said it in a minute without thinking. And now I don't know what to do. He has gone to London."

"I don't know what you mean, James. He has gone to London, we all know tint, and we may pretty well guess that he isn't doing much good there." "And if I had kept my promise he might have been here, and doing well." "Not he; he has never done well yet."

"t he 'might have done now. Ile has never had a very good chance." James said, and stopped short, surprised to find that excuse upon his lips. "I shall have to go to him," he presently went on. "Go to him! What for Don't, pray, go to seek the companionship of Leo Stevens." "I must try and set the thing right," "flight! It is right enough that Mr. Westerall should know the truth, and right enough that Stevens should bear the responsibility of his own acts." "I must go and try," said James. f Towards midday he found himself seeking, in a m streetean andt h a t dingy, for oo r his e country-bredabod eoLeo Stevens; and eyes singularly by-and-bye was admitted to an untiay sitting-room, where Leo, looking also untidy and uncared for, lounged on a shabby sofa. He turned his bead lazily at the opening of the door, then, with a sharp change from his listlessness to anger, sat upright, and said: " You " James ca.me.forward. Assuredly any sins of which he may have been guilty towards Leo received in that moment their full measure of punishment. "I have come-" he said, and stopped. "I wanted-" He stopped again. "Go on," said Leo. "I should like to know what you do want." The task of the repentant Pharisee grew hard, before him. He had come full of sympathetic warmth for the fellow sinner, burdened like himself with a knowledge of faultiness. But when he came face to face with Leo the old distrust, the ola antagonism. sprang up. He stood longing with the whore force of his soul to express himself, and knowing that he would be unable. "I came to see you because of Mr. Westerall," he said at last. _ .

"I should have thought," Leo retorted, "that you had done enough for me in that quarter. My affairs are no concern of Mr. Westerall's, I am happy to say." "No, but you know I told him—" "I know what you told him well enough," Leo interrupted, standing up and turning from him. "That's all right enough. You saintly people aren't bound to keep faith with sinners. And I might have robbed a safe or two, mightn't I? What is it to you if I starve? But I tell you what, I'll starve my own way, without you or any of the lot of you coming to spy on me." . "But I want you to come back, said James. "What do you mean?" "Come back to Greyshatn. It is not too late to do well yet. What are you—six-and-twenty?" The remembrance of his own words to Emily came across him: "A man who has done no good till he is six-and-twenty is not likely to begin then." He looked at Leo and felt them true. "Come back to Greysham ? Westerall won't take me now. You don't suppose I'll go back with this story known against me everywhere?" went criedto Leo, m "But .1 think Mr. Westerall would take vou e , p **With a sudden flash of hope. Then, relaxing, he shook his head hopelessly. "Let me try, at any rate. If it hadn't been for er, hpaop s y—Oilfl me you would have 'teen with him, doing well— perhaps." • Leo caught up the last hesitating clause. "1 should—l should! And now there's no chance for me; and that's your doing." James stood awkwardly, feeling, indeed, some pity, but also, and far more intensely, a powerful disinclination to have anything to do with Leo. He could not bring himself to say frankly: "It was my doing, and I ask your pardon," although that was precisely what he had come to London in order to say. He did manage to say: "But I hope you have a chance, and I'll do what I can to help you." Ile words sounded to himself horribly con- lescenclitig, but he could not contrive to make them more cordial. "Things have always been against mei* said Leo, dropping himself on the sofa once more. "It has always been Give a dog a bad flame.'" Ile hung bis arm over the sofa-end and laid his face upon it. James, who, under no conceivable circumstances. would have exhibited- such a mood of limp despair, stood gazing at him blankly. In a moment Leo lifted hii head and said: "I'll

Thm, indeed, James was moved. "That book in MaHinson's" was the book which Leo had been accused, at school, of taking from a companion's desk an selling, and James had never doubted the verdict of the school against him. Now be saw in a flash the suspected' lad's sense of wrong and injury—the feeling that endeavour ,was useless in the face Of a fixed prejudice. And he knew also —or felt at that moment as if he knew—that if he had believed in Leo, Leo would have stayed on in Greythait and worn the prejudice out. "Do you believe that?" Leo asked, watching him.

"I do," said James, and took Leo's limp hand into a firm grasp. It was the first time their hands had met since Leo had come to confess a wrong done to James, and each of them knew it was the first time.

On his return to Greysham between fivP and Si. o'clock, James made straight for Mr. Westerall's house. The first object upon which his eyes fell within the house door was an umbrella belonging to his father, and for a moment he had thoughts of turning back. But it is difficult to take flight from a respectful servant holding open a door, and he went in.

In the drawing-room he found the two seniors. Emily, and a ravaged tea-table. Fresh tea was brought in for him. and Emily, fixed to the duties of her post, cut-him to the heart by making polite conversation for him while' the elders talked- that gossip of business which passes with the outsider for serious. When Emily rose from her table she directed her attention rather pointedly to Mr. Southgate, and presently led him to look at some new products of her conservatory. James noted the avoidance of himself, and gave it a sigh, and then turned to her uncle.

"I wanted to sp—'eak to you, Mr.. Westerall, about Leo Stevens. I have been up to see him." "Have you?" said Mr. Westerall, with an air of great indifference. "It was only what I told you, was it not, that determined you?" Westerall seemed to hesitate, looked up and down as though to ask: "Why am I thus catechised?" and finally replied: "No, no; nothing else."

"Then will you forget that I said it? It was three years ago. I believe—l hope that he would do better now. I shall never forgive myself for having destroyed his last chance—and when I had promised." The last words dropped. He could scarcely bring himself to the acknowledgment. "What do you mean?" Mr. Westerall demanded. raising himself upright in his chair and turning with, solemn severity to confront the speaker. "Yell ask me, knowing what I know, to take that young vagabond into my bank! I am perfectly astonished—perfectly." "But I had no right to say it at all," James per- sisted. In his solemnity Mr. Westerall had spoken loudly and distinctly. Emily and Mr. Southgate, just within the conservatory, paused. "But you had a right—you had a duty!" Mr. Westerall cried, bringing down an emphatic hand on the arm of his chair. Mr. Southgate stepped forward into the room, and stood almost behind his son. Emily lingered in the doorway. "And so you mean to say you have been in London looking after that reprobate, and wanting to bring him back into my bank!" Mr. Westera,ll proceeded, in a tone of increasing anger. "I told you how it would be," said Mr. Southgate at his son's ear. "Did you eier hear such a thing?" Mr. Westerall demanded, turning from the son to the father. "Na right to tell me, indeed !'t James stood up: a blank despair came upon him, a mute indignation rendered all the more bitter by his perception that it was unavailing against the world and fate. He turned away in silence, and found himself face to face with Emily. Her eyes were shining, her whole face alight with unusual emotion. She took his hand and held it fast. For a moment she did not swak. Then, with the superior s6lf- command—by the superficial deemed duplicity—of woman, she recovered herself and said: "Come and look at my ferns." The two young people passed together into the conservatory, and Emily shut the door behind them, The two elders looked at each other and smiled. ”I suppose it was to please Emily," said the uncle. "I suppose it was," said the father. And perhaps they were right, although James woul4 have been of a different opinion. (THE END.]

FIRE AT A WARRINGTON WORKS. Wheelwright's Premises in Flames.

Damage to the extent of several hundreds of pounds was caused by a fire which occurred on the premises of Mr. James H. Heywood, wheelwright, Rose and Crown street on Wednesday morning. P.C. Rylatt and Mr. W. Russell, cab proprietor,wereyi Bridge street at 6.25 on Wednesday morning whrn they perceived a smell of burning material. Mr. Russell went into the yard of the Higher Seven Stars Inn and saw that an outbreak of fire had occurred on Mr. Heywood's premises. The constable immediately ran to the fire bell and rang it. The brigade turned out smartly with the steam fire engine Majorand a full complement of men in charge of Super- intendent Turner. On arrival they found a building 70ft long by 20ft %vide,with a corrugated iron roof in flames One portion of the building contained a stock of well seasoned timber fora wheelwright's business,whilst Immediately adjoining was a smith's shop and an engine room filled with pmchinery. In addition to two gas engines there were in the building a circular saw bench, boring machine, two drilling machines. an emery machine with a large amount of shafting and pulleys. Three or four hydrants were immediately got to work and these were augmented by three powerful jets from the steam fire engine. After one and a half hours' work the firemen succeeded in subduing the flames and saved the adjoining property, including a large workshop, which was fortunately separated from the scene of the fire by ' a party wall. Immediately behind an adjoining wall, a small joiner's shop in Mr. Bolton's brev%ery yard was practically gutted and the tools destroyed. Considerable trouble was experienced with a three inch gas main, the fire having burnt it off 41 the main side of the meter. Over an hour elapsed before the iron main could be reached and the escape of gas stopped. When the fire had been subdued two or three men were left on the premises to keep the smouldering d4bris in check. The damage, roughly estimated at several hundred pounds, is covered by insurance.

MINISTER MUST KISS THE LADIES.

Rev. Job Urquhart, Glasgow, preaching at Highgate- road Baptist Chapel, London, on Sunday, denounced the present-day church methods and teaching. He could mention churches, he said, in which a congregation could not be obtained unless the minister kissed all the young ladies.

EVENTS OF THE WEEK.

THr\VAR IN THE FAR EAST.

Port Arthur has taken - the Japanese longer to capture than they originally , anticipated, but now it looks as though the great Russian fortress were on its last legs. General Stoessel has made a most gallrnt defence, but little by little the Japanese have been drawing the cordon closer with re- lentless persistence, regardless of cost in men, money and munitions. The capture of Wolf's Hill completed the ring round Port Arthur, and since then it has been raked by a fearful fire from all sides. In consequence the harbour became untenable, and a sortie of the fleet took place at the end of last week with the object of either getting clear away or joining Admiral Skrydloff's squadron at Vladivostock. Admiral Witgert led the sortie with the Tsarevitch, and with the help of the Retvisan endeavoured to draw the fire of the Japanese fleet, whilst the other battleships and fast cruisers got away. But Admiral Togo was ready for him, and the Russians were badly defeated and sadly knocked about. Six battleships and four cruisers left Port Arthur, and of them five battleships and one cruiser were driven back

GENERAL STOESSEL. ADMIRAL WITGERT. into the harbour in a crippled condition. The other battleship, the Tsarewitch, with the cruiser Diana and a torpedo-destroyer got away to the German port of Kiaochan, -where they will have to be dismantled for entering a neutral harbour. The Tsarevitch was practically disabled in the fight, her -engines being injured and her steering gear shot away, while she lost two hundred killed, including Admiral Witgert, who was blown to pieces, and sixty wounded. The Askold got to Woosung, but the whereabouts -of the Novik is uncertain. The Retshitelny got to Chifu, where she was dismantled by the Chinese, but shortly afterwards the Japanese entered the harbour and boarded her. A serious fight ensued, and in the end the Japanese towed her out. It is obvious that some misunderstanding has Occurred, as the Japanese action was a distinct breach of neutrality, and Germany and France have made strong representations to China, which in turn has made a grave protest to Japan.

THE RELEASE ON EX-LIEUTENANT WITTON.

The King's clemency in pardoning Lieut. "Wilton, who was serving a sentence of penal servitude for life at Portland, has created -widespread interest and has met with general acclamation. Few cases of-recent years have -mused such keen public sympathy, and it was felt that if justice required satisfaction at all by now all her claims had been paid in full. The case of Lieut. Witton was one of -the many arising out of the recent war in South Africa, but it was of all the most in- teresting, being shrouded in considerable mystery. The WITTON. young officer in question is an Australian who -went to the front in one of the Colonial -corps and was given a commission in the Bushveldt Carbineers. Three years ago, -whilst on service, the officers of the corps found the body of Captain Hunt, one of their most popular comrades, on the veldt mutilated and robbed. It was near Opelon- ker, near Pietersburg, and they swore to -avenge him. Capturing a Boer who was actually wearing some of Captain Hunt's -clothes and in possession of his water-bottle and other things, Lieuts. Morant and Han- cock had him shot and ordered other prisoners to be treated in the same way. This came to the ears of some other -prisoners under escort .of Lieut. Witton, who had protested against his superior officer's orders., and one of them went for him. In self-defence he shot him dead. As the result of a court-martial, Lieuts. Hancock and Morant were shot, Lieut. Witton sentenced to penal servitude for life, and Lieut. Picton cashiered. Now after serving three years Lieut. Wit ton has been released. When interviewed, he refused to pass any comments on his trial and the circumstances of the case.

A PROMINENT FRENCH STATESMAN. Few men can claim to have served their

country better than M. Waldeck- Rousseau, the late Premier of France, who passed away last week after a long and painful illness, which culminated in an operation, which he had not the strength to survive. M. Waldeck-Rousseau was, however, a comparatively young man, being under sixty years of age, but he had lived stren- uously. He took office at the early age of thirty-five, and since then he was always the most prominent figure in the French Re- public. He had only been a member of the Chamber of Deputies two years when he took office under Gambetta, and on his fall, two years later, M. Jules Ferry, in recon- structing, gave him the portfolio of the Interior. Meanwhile, M. Waldeck-Rousseau removed his legal practice from Rennes to Paris, and soon acquired a great reputation in the French capital. When not in office he was reputed to be making from it 2,000 to it 5,000 n year at the Bar, and he would acce• -der ten thousand

THE NORTH-EAST LANARK ELECTIOW.

As the result of the bye-election in, North- East Lanark, another seat has been won by the Liberals, who converted a minority of 904 into a majority of 942. The vacancy was caused by the unfortunate death of Sir William Ratigan in a motor accident a few weeks ago, and the election was very keenly fought. The candidates were Provost Find- lay (Liberal), Mr. G. R. Touch (Unionist and Tariff Reformer), and Mr. J. R. Robert- son (Labour), and the poll was an exception- ally heavy one, 14,280 voters out of 18,922 registering their votes. The result was that the • Liberal and the Unionist candidates practically changed places in the number of their votes to within a few, whilst the Labour candidate showed an increase of a thousand. The Liberal candidate was sup- ported by the Irish and the temperance party, but a large proportion of the six thousand miners in the constituency plumped for Mr. Robertson. The new member, Mr.

PROVOST FINDLAY.

Alexander Findlay, resides at Bellfield House, Motherwell, and was born at Irvine, Ayr- shire, in 1844. In early life he was appren- ticed to a pattern-maker, and then he emigrated to America, returning after five years• in the States. .He next spent some time in Glasgow prior to removing to Motherwell, and it was not till 1888 that he started business on his own account as Alexander Findlay and Co., manufacturing steel roofs, bridges, and .all structural ap- paratus. Four years ago it was turned , into a company under the managing directorship of Mr. Findlay, and it is now one of the greltest employers of labour in the neigh- bourhood. Mr. Colville, M.P. for North- East Lanark, from 1895 till his death in 1901, was one of the directors. Mr. Findlay. who has been connected with the Motherwell Burgh Commission for over twenty years, has been Provost since 1901.

THE BRITISH AMBASSADORSHIP AT PARIS.

The most desired of all diplomatic posi- tions—that of Ambassador Extraordinary and Plenipotentiary to the French Republic—is shortly to become vacant by the retirement of Sir Edmund Monson, who is now within three months of completing his 70th year. This, it may be stated, is not Si r Edmund's first resignation, for, thirty-nine years ago, after only nine years of diplomatic service, he re- signed his posi- tion as third Secretary t the Embassy in Hanover, in- tending to enter Mon a Parlia- liEntary career. However, h e was defeated at the poll whilst SIR EDMUND MONSON. standing fo r Reigate. He had already been a Fellow of All Souls Col- lege, Oxford, for seven years. He became examiner for Taylorian Scholarships, but in 1869 again determined to represent his country abroad, and accepted the position of Consul• in the Azores. Two years later he became Consul-General in Hungary, and soon afterwards was appointed Second Secre- tary to the Vienna Embassy. In the trou- blous times of 1876 he did special service in Dalmatia and Montenegro, with the result that • the C.B. was conferred upon him. He then represented Great Britain in Uru- guay, in the Argentine Republic, and in Paraguay up to 1884, and later in Denmark, Greece, Belgium and Austria, until in 1893, his important work in Paris began. Few readers may now remember the enterprising little society journal, " Echoes from the Clubs," which flourished some thirty-five years ago, but those who do ni iy know that Sir Edmund was one of the leading con. tributors.

THE NEW CZAREVITCH,

Whole-hearted congratulations have been showered upon the Czar and Czarina of Russia by the birth of a son and heir. It is nearly ten years since the Czar was mar- ried to a Princess of Hesse, a niece of King Edward, and the superstitious Russian peasant, painfully impressed by the disaster which attended the Czar's coronation had begun lately to regard the Czarina as the hostile influence in Imperial fortunes. The four daughters who have been born to the Imperial House count for nothinc, in Russian eyes, and the familiar prophecy of the sorceress who told the Czar that he would be the father of six daughters in succession has been quoted with serious faith and mournful anticipations on each occasion of a daughter being born. With the possible ex- ception of the Grand Duke Michael, Hier- Presumptive to the thorne in default of a son being born t- Czar, there was

THE CZARINA.

probably no man or woman in Russia who did not openly regard the birth of each of these daughters, in 1895, 1897, 1899, and 1901, as a vast national misfortune. Great festivities have been taking place all over the Russian Empire in honour of the auspicious event. Two Imperial rescripts have been issued, one of the subject of the birth, and the other granting a pardon to prisoners. The Czar is said to have expressed his own personal desire that in humble recognition of this mercy of God all political prisoners should have their sentences much reduced. Twenty-two titles will be conferred on the little prince, and he will be the chief of a new order of nobility. Whatever, the future may have in store for Russia in the • contest with Japan there is no gainsaying the fact that the birth of a son to the Romanoff dynasty has come at a moment when a revolution was imminent, and when Royalty dare only be spoken of in whisper.

THEM RAPII3 TRANSIT. —Hand in hand they strolled in the quiet evening, happy in themselves and their surroundings. Suddenly the girl halted and said: "Percy, what is that?" Percy, without releasing her hand, stopped, and said: "Gladys darling, it's a snail." And so they wandered on for a sweet half-hour longer, when the maiden again said: Percy, look, there's another snail." Percy again bent his manly form, and looking up replied: "No, Glayds, 'tis the same snail." A CAT STOR.Y.—" My wife had a cat, and I hated him. When he was dead, I took him in the yard and buried him two feet deep. " When my wife came home I couldn't keep still, and, like a fool, up and told her. 'Tom was gone.' Well, there was no sleep for me that night. The next day the old woman cut up so rough I got scared, and, thinking to divert any possible sus- picion from myself, put an advertisement in the paper offering a big reward for Tom's return. This made a hit with the old lady." "Next day about one Hundred cats were brought to the house. As I would let the men in and tell my wife to go and take a look at the cats, I nearly laughed my- self to death thinking what a joke I had about that reward." Here my friend lapsed into melan- choly thought. I stirred him up by asking him how it ended. "How did it end? My wife iden- tified one of those cats as her Tom I"

A CURE FOR BALKING —I was riding along a mountain road in East Kentucky (writes a travel- ler), when I saw a mule running towards me with a singletree dangling at his heels. With great difficulty I got out ot his way. About a mile far- ther on I saw two front wheels and the waggon box. I looked round to see if the driver had been hurt; but, finding no one I drove on. In a few minutes I met a man walking rather quickly down the road. "Stranger," he asked, "did you see a mule down that road?" "Yes." "Did he hey a rag over his ear?" "I didn't see any." "Waal, it's all right. I reckon 'e'll stop when 'e gets flustered out, an' I reckon 'e's cured." "What is he cured of?" I asked. " Balkin'. You see, I heerd that a grasshopper put in th' ear o' a hoes or mule 'd cure 'im from balkin'. So I tied a rag over the critter's ear so it couldn't get out, cotched a grasshopper, put 'im in, an', stranger, it's the best remedy 1 ever seed. Th' mule didn't give me no time to get in th' waggon. I never seed a mule so sprightly. I reckon th' hopper's got out now, an' I'll go and cotch the mule.'

A-GOOD DoG.---Fair Customer: "I live in the suburbs, and I want, a good house-dog." Dealer: "Yes, mum." "But, of course, I don't want one that will keep us awake all night barking at nothing." "No, mum." "He must be big and strong and fierce, you know. "Yes, mum." "Yet as gentle as a lamb with us, you know." "Yes, mum." "And he must pounce on every tramp that comes along amj drive him off." "Yes, mum." But he mustn't interfere with any poor but honest man looking for work." "No, mum." "If a burglar comes prowling about at night the dog should make mincemeat of him in an instant." "Yes, mum." "But he mustn't attack a neighbour who drops in for an evening call." "No, mum." "And, of course, he mustn't molest people who come hurrying in at all hours of the night to call my husband. He's the doctor, you know." "No, mum. I see what you want. You want a thought-reader dog." "Yes. I sup- pose so." "Very sorry, mum, but I'm out of that kind of dog."

MOTOR CAR ON THE TOP OF A TREE. An automobile touring the Catskill Mountains became unmanageable on Monday, and running down a steep embankment, fell safely on the top of a huge apple-tree. The four passengers, who were uninjured, descended by a ladder. The tree was cut down,and the machine finally extricated. The New York correspon- dent of the" Daily Telegraph "wires the news.

THE LATE M. CiALDECK-ROUSSEAU.

tancs. In 1894 he again entered the political world upon his election to the Senate, and in 1899, at the time of the national crisis over the Dreyfus case, President Loubet and patriotism forced him to accept the Premiership. Never will it be forgotten in what a thoroughly masterly -way, he tackled the case which was causing little short of civil war throughout the coun- try, and to his policy of probity was due the second trial at Rennes. The Dreyfus .case settled, the great lawyer turned his attention to the other great French scandal, the Humbert case, and with equal Pluck he cleared up the whole fraud. But the most remarkable act of his administra- tion was the passing of the Law on Asso- Fations, which his successor, M. Combes, no carrying out so vigorously.

CHESHIRE'S EDUCATION BUDGET.

The Cheshire Education Committee have had laid before them a report in regard to the allocation of the funds available for higher education in the county for the year ending the 31st March next. The allocation of the amounts under the customs and excise duties, with the proceeds of a penny rate raised in the respective areas, will give the boroughs and districts the following sums :—Borough of Congleton, £372; Crewe, £1,611 : Dukinfield, £709; Hyde, £1,240; Macclesfield, £1,217; Stalybridge, £1,086: urban district of Wallasey, £2,624; Altrincham, Bowdon, and Hale districts, £1,524; Ashton and Stockport Union areas, £1,749; Bebington and Neston district, 41,350; Chester Union area, £850; Congleton Union areas £1,062; Hoylake and West Kirby district, £819: Knutsford and Wilmslow district, £943; Lymm district, £896; Macclesfield and Hayfield Union areas, £1,365 ; Nantwich Union area, £1,583; North- wich district, £1,624; Runcorn rural area, £1.195; Run- corn urban district, £620; Sale and Ashton-underr-Lyne district, £1,032; Winsford and Middlewich district, £BBB.

SNEEZING WITH PINS IN THE MOUTH.

The danger of the habit indulged in by ladies of dressing. while holding pins in their mouths was illus- trated m New York this week. Miss Rose Biggis seized with an irresistible desire to sneeze, swallowed two pins. A surgical operation was necessary, but the lady has died.

PASSIVE RESISTERS AT STOCKTON

HEATH.

Onen-air Meeting on the Square.

The Rev. J. S. Hughes and Mr. Law—two of the passive resisters whose watches were sold in the War- rington Market Place last weak, in consequence of ; their refusal to pay the Education rat* owing to their conscientious objection to support sectarian teaching in elementary schools—addressed an open-air meeting in Victoria Square,Stockton Heath on Monday evening. The Rev. H. Dunnico and the• Rev. J. F. Matthews were unable to be present. A large number of villagers and sympathisers gathered round and listened atten- tively to the reasons which the speakers advanced for the attitude they had taken up. Standing on a block of stone near the Police Station, the Rev. J. S. Hughes opened the proceedings with a few words of prayer, and then called upon Mi.. H. 'Law to.peak...

111. r. H.Law observed that they were there that night to utter their public protest against the Education Bill of 1902. It was an unjust measure and they were deter- mined to do all they could to get the law amended. (Hear, hear.) As passive resisters they were not usually disregardful of the claims of the law, but on the other hand they were prepared to do all they could to main- tain a good and righteous law.. (Hear, hear.) This particular Education Bill was an utter violation of one of the first principles of representative government—a violation of the principle that wherever you imposed taxation there should be corresponding representation. (Hear, hear.) The Government professed to have recog- nised this all important consideration but they had only provided that the public representatives on the Educa- tion authority should be one-third. The people's representatives were always in a minority. They might raise their voioes as often as they liked against anything proposed to be done on the Board but they were sure to be outvoted and he submitted it was an insult to any intelligent man to be asked to serve on any Board under such conditions. Another serious and unfair feature of the bill was that although the education of the children was provided for and the salaries of teachers paid out of the taxes of the people, there were 16,000 head-teaclaerships which were available only to men and women who were prepared to teach sectarian and doctrinal tenets. In these scheols it meant that the first consideration was not an educa- tional qualification but the one of being prepared to propagate denominational teaching in the schools. This was an injustice to the teaching profession and might lead some of those who applied for positions to stifle the voice of conscience and accept the conditions attached rather than run the risk of losing the place. Such conditions were sure to be prejudicial to the interests of the school. From a moral standpoint the teachers would lose in tone owing to the fact that they were teaching that which their consciences did not approve. There was another grave objection—in his opinion the most grievous—that was the appropriation of taxes for denominational teaching. (Hear, hear.) They were not there to run down any religi- ous denomination whatever. The Anglican and the Catholic had a perfect right to their I own convictions of truth. As Nonconformists they respected those convictions as sincerely and as devoutly as they claimed respect for their own convictions. (Hear, hear.) But these denominations had no right to put their hand in his pocket to make him pay for the teaching of that which he regarded as error. One could scarcely believe that in this 20th century, after all the struggles for freedom and religious liberty, any government would dare to rob them of so precious a heritage. Mr. Law proceeded to refer to the martyrdom of Latimerand Ridley at Oxford,l aad salad the Education Bill bore a striking resemblance to the narrow, bigoted, intolerant and coercive legisla- tion of Latimer's days. They were not there as anti- educationalists but they advocated the best education possible for the children and they were not unwilling to pay for it. They did not object to the education rate but to the sectarian portion of it. (Hear, hear). Over 20,000 resisters had had their goods distrained upon rather than pay this rate and 25 more had preferred to go to gaol rather than pay, amongst the number being the Rev. Thomas Chatnpness, a gentleman over 70 years of age and a true veteran of Nonconformity. Surely there must be something radically wrong about a bill when they found men mustering in their thousands against it. Their hope was that when an appeal was made to the country, they would have a House of Commons of a very different complexion to the present one, who would hasten to amend those features of the bill which violated the consciences of so many thousands of people of every religious persuasion. (Hear, hear). The Rev. J. S. Hughes said they did not deny that they were resisting the law of the land in orde- to make their protest against what they considered a monstrous Set of Parliament. If he could have had his way they would have had the sale of their precious watches in that parish and he believed they would have had an even larger gathering than they had that night. In relating his first appearance in a police court, Mr. Hughes said although he regarded himself as a respectable citizen, he received a summons to appear at the Petty Sessions. He could not say that they %ere very kindly received I in that court. In fact he was inclined to think that there was more coldness manifested than there ought to .have been, although there was one of the magistrates who undoubtedly had a friendly feeling towards them. A very different reception was given in a court at Hampstead to the Rev. Dr. Horton. He was permitted to speak and to give a very able and eloquent reason for the attitude that he had taken up. Although they had waited at Daresbury Sessions over an hour there came over the magistrates a strange zeal to save time. Next time he did not think they would ask permission to speak. Even a common criminal who pleaded guilty had a right to say ie word in self-defence, but the Daresbury magistrates did not accord to those who had never been in a police court before the common right given to a common criminal. In addition to the appearance in the Police Court, he had the bailiffs in his house for the first tune in his life. (Laughter.) He did not offer a mean sacrifice when he give the bailiff his watch for it was one that he greatly prized, inasmuch as it was given to him by his father when he entered college. He supposed it was bought in at the sale' for he had received it back since. He (Mr. Hughes) was the only one of the three passive resisters who had suffered disfranchisement. His name did not appear on the list of voters, but he intended to appear before the Revising Barrister and claim it. Dr. Massey, of Oxford. had stated that disfranchisement on the ground of unpaid poor-rate could only be carried out by tricky delay in the way of distraint. He (Mr. Hughes) ought to have been summoned six months ago. As passive resisters they took their stand because conscience compelled it. He had the honour to represent a denomination, one of whose vital principles was that religion must be voluntarily supported. It was con- trary to their deepest convictions and to their practice to recognise state interference in matters of religion. The addition to the education rate was distinctly for the purpose of paying for such religion in our day schools. (Hear, hear.) As long as the voluntary schools paid 2s. 6d. in the pound towards the maintenance of their work they had some sort of right to call them- selves voluntary schools. But when they did not pay a farthing, and called upon the citizen to pay the whole of the money, the schools could no longer be called voluntary schools. They became national schools and he desired that they should be truly national and know neither denomination, sect or party. Believing as he did in the non-intervention of the State with religion he could not pay a rate to support denominational teach- ing in schools. This question of the state control of religion was at the bottom of their objection, and he believed it would do much to hasten the time when they would have the disestablishment—and very likely the disendowraent of the church. They not only objected to the principle of state control but they objected to the kind of teaching in these sectarian schools. Even in schools in the neighbourhood where the masters must know that there are Baptist children in attendance they went through the catechism and the children were taught to say : "Who gave you that name ?—My God, father and my God-mother at my baptism." That was repeated although some of the children had never beenibaptised and although they never hadOod-fathers and God-mothers. It might be said that they had the conscience clause. But why should there ben conscience clause in a national school which belonged to the nation? (Hear, hear.) They did not want such patronage or privilege; they wanted equal rights, and equal rights they must have. This battle must be fought to the bitter end—or rather the blessed end—for it would be a blessed end for this country when they were delivered from sectarianism in the day school. The recent Educa- tion Act was grossly unjust towards Nonconformist teachers. It prevented Nonconformist teachers in day schools from attaining the positions of head-masters and head mistresses in no less than 8,000 schools. This disability was not because they were unable to teach, because :Nonconformist teachers were amongst the best teachers of the country, but because they would not sign the 39 articles of the Church of England or would not submit to religious tests. A teacher in a national school was a national servant, and a national servant ought to be free from all religious tests. Was it right that a Nonconformist who happened to secure a position in the school could never be anything but an assistant master? He contended that all the positions in our national schools ought to be open to all the young people without regard to their religion. Let them be promoted by merit and not barred by any religious test. (Hear, hear.) Since the universities had been thrown open to Nonconformists, the far greater number of senior wranglers had been Nonconformists. Religious tests had been removed by the constant struggle which Nonconformists of all sections had carried on, and the Nonconformists of the present day were going to continue this battle until all that was obnoxious, unjust, and contrary to the highest reason was swept out of this education act. (Hear, hear.) He appealed to those present to help • them in this great work. (Applause.) Mr. Law and Mr. Hughes invited questions, but none were asked.

In closing the meeting, Mr. Hughes said the only way to settle this question was by giving to the in- habitants of the district full control of the schools., (Hear, hear.) Referring to religious teaching in schools Mr. Hughes said he was in favour of the Bible being read but was against sectarian teaching:

===== 0002601_19040820_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====

A WARRINGTON YOUTH'S DOWNFALL.

Robs His Employers and Proceeds to

Canada.

The story of a Warrington youth's downfall was prosaically told in the Warrington Borough Police Court on Saturday before Mr. Birettall and other magis- trates. George Allen Cooper, described as a book- keeper, appeared in the dock to answer a charge of stealing on May 17 and 19, a sum of money in cash amounting to £4O, the property of Messrs. Boydell Bros., tailors, Sankey-street, by whom he had been employed. He is 18 years of age, of respectable parents in residence at Stockton Heath. He appeared to feel his position acutely, and pleaded guilty in a low voice. Mr. Steel appeared on his behalf. _ -

Superintenaent Monaghan detailed the facts of the case. Cooper, he said, was formerly a book-keeper to Messrs. Boydell at their Warrington shop, occupying the position for some twelve months. On May 17th he was given about LIO in cheques and gold to take to Parr.s Bank. Two days later he was given a further sum of £23 to bank. It afterwards appeared that he merely banked the cheques, keeping the gold (Z 46 in his possession. He made an entry in the cash book as having paid in the whole amount. At the week end he gave his notice in. He received his wages on the Satur- day, but never turned up again, leaving Liverpool for Canada, in company with two other young men, on the Monday. He remained in Canada for a short time, but did not meet with the success he had expected, and, consequently returned home a few days ago. He went to Messrs. Boydell's Manchester office, and there made a full confession of everything. He was handed over to the Manchester police, who datained him until the arrival of a police officer from Warrington. He handed over a bank pass book which he took with him, the absence of which had caused considerable trouble to the firm. •

Mr. Bryan, Messrs. Boydell's head cashier, gave the results of his investigations into the accounts. He found that £46 in gold which the prisoner should have deposited in the bank had not been paid in. The prisoner confessed taking the money,but his employers could not pass over a matter like that. Mr. Steel, solicitor, in appealing for the mercy of the Court, said he did so at the request of the broken- hearted father and mother. He wished the task had been in other hands, as Coorer was once in his employ as clerk. He left to better himself at the St. Helens Cable Company's works, and had a very good written character given hini sthen he left there to go to Messrs. Boydell's. He was only 18 years of age, but appeared to have got under the influence of bad companions who unfortunately had led him astray. Two of these com- panions suggested that he might be able to get money to go to Canada with them, and, acting on the sudden impulse when he had such a large amount in his possession, he did rob his employers. The three young fellows left for Canada, hoping to make a fortune, but likeso many others they found out their mistake. When all the money was spent they were stranded. Cooper wrote pitiful letters home telling them how he had had to pawn part of his clothing for food, and that he and his companions were absolutely stranded. "Oh well," he continued, "God pays his debts without money, and here we are, in a dirt,.state, beggars, and God knows what we shall do." in a further letter, Cooper stated that he had decided to work his passage home to London,butt that he di,-I not know what he would do when he got there. He really did not think he would see the old home again. He read those extracts, Mr. Steel said, to show the state of mind the prisoner had got into, and the trials he had undergone urged on by the influence and sug- gestions of his bad companions.who had induced him to take this money and go with them to Canada. As an instance of how he telt the wrong he had done he might say that in one letter prisoner said" I have sinned greatly, and I don't know whether life is really worth living." Eventually he worked his passage to London, and, as he was again stranded in London, his parents sent him money to come home by train." He was sure their worships were kindly-hearted men, who had no desire to make a gaol bird of the prisoner if it could be avoided. His character was now practically gone,and making him a criminal in the proper sense would probably cause him to think it was no good try- ing to be better, and he would sink lower and lower in the social scale. He asked them to consider all the circumstances—the broken-hearted father and mother, prisoner's previous good character, the evil companions, , the temptation of having so much money in his hands, the trials and sufferings his folly had cost him, his re- turn home broken in mind and spirit, his free and full confession, and finally, his resolve to live an honourable life in the future. He thought they would feel that it was a case where they might show sympathy and be lenient. He would suggest, if he might, that they should sentence the prisoner to one day's imprisonment, and then he would not have to go to Strangeways and become a gaol bird. The magistrates had not power to deal with him under the First Offenders' Act, but he submitted it was a case in which the magistrates might show sympathy and leniency. The Chairman (Mr. Birchall) in giving the decision of the magistrates, said they felt the case could not be passed over so lightly as the prisoner's kind-hearted advocate had suggested. However, they would deal with it leniently and sentence prisoner to a month's imprisonment in the second class, which meant that his term would be without hard labour, and that he would I not be mixed up with the ordinary offenders.
