The address MR. BALFOUR'S delivered at Carn- METAPHYSICS. bridge onWednesday by the Prime 111inis ter will not be found very easy reading. Mr. BALFOUR is believed by his admirers to wield a light pen. There is, indeed, an apparent lightness about his style; it his meanings are usually so elusive that, as in this instance, what he writes makes uncommonly heavy reading. Sir WALTER SCOTT once asked for something craggy to break his mind upon. He might have found it in Mr. BALFOUR'S address to the British Association had he now been alive. C ,nfessing that he is no great hand at physics, the Prime Minister betakes himself to metaphysics. His discourse is entitled " R9flections on the New Theory of Hatter." What is the new theory of matter It is something that is as yet in a rather incohate condition, like Mr. FALFOUR'S views on the subject of Free Trade. The discovery of radium and of radio-activity have ma le it necessary to bring under review for purposes of revision almost every, established theory of the universe. The atom has lost the respect formerly paid t( it. The discovery is now made that it is not the unit of matter ; that it is not a thing in itself ; that, in fact, it is a system of electrical monads, whi•3h may he only a modification of the universally pervading ether. It is on reflections suggested by the new discoveries that Mr. BALFOUR'S mind has been at work while his col- leagues have been ernWoyad on the Licensing Bill and the preparation of schemes for the coercion of Wales.

In the early portion of bib address Mr. BALFOUR corrects what he calls a linguistic error. He , corrects the view that it is a knowledge of Nature's laws that we are seeking when we investigate !Nature's ;corks. What we are really in search of is somethiug on which to frame a conception of the physi4l universe in its inner reality. Now, of late years the scientific conception of what is real has been quite phenomenally changed. We have been living among illusions, he observes. Even the methods of investigation have had to be changed. It has been commonly believed that he who would search out the secrets of Nature must humbly wait on experience. Bat, says Mr. tBALFOUR, that is only partly true. "Now and again," he goes on to say, "it happens that observation and experiment are not treated as guides to be meekly followed, but as witnesses to be broken doWn in cross-examination." Whether or not he approves of this new method of scientific inquiry,which seems a little violent and unsafe, is not wholly clear. Toe address is full of all manner of if ifs." "If the dust beneath our feet be indeed compounded of innumerable systems." "If gross matter be a grouping of atoms, and if atoms be a system of electrical monads, what" ---and so on. Mr. BALFOUR'S questionings, like the farmer's claret, get us "no forrarder." Some of those who read his address will be shocked to find him disrespectfully of the law of Gravitation, callingp mind, perhaps, the long familiar but not s eak in entirely tgoaccurate lines :

- The very law that moulds a tear, And bids it trickle from its source— That law preserves the earth a sphere, And guides the planets in their course. "Gravitation," t i says Mr. BALFOUR, "is trifling in npath the attractions and repulsions h which i o l'il we are familiar bbtween electrically- cw 3rsiacoh charged bodies." If the Prime Minister has a settled scientific conviction it is, evidently, that electricity is the basis of all things, the inner reality of the universe. The speculations of the Cambridge address have no very remote likeness to those of Novella and the earlier mystics. Do we live in a real world? Are we ourselves realitie,i? Or is the whole universe merely an arpearance ? By the new

theory as to matter, says Mr. BALFOUR, "matter is not ynerely explained, but explained away." Then he pledges himself to come to the point to which he desires to call attention. It is something "not to be sought in the great divergence between matter as conceived by the physicist and matter as the ordinary man supposes himself to know it, between matter as it is perceived and matter as it really is, but to the fact that the first of these two vite inconsistent views is wholly based on the second. We claim:to found all our scientific opinions on experience; and the exper- ience on which we found our theories of the physicals univerise is our sense-perception of that universe. That is experience and in this region of belief there is no other. Yet the conclusions which thus profess to be entirely founded upon experience are to all appearance fundamentally opposed to it; our knowledge of reality is based upon allusion, and the very conceptions we , use in describing it to others, or in think- ing of it ourselves, are abstracted from anthro- pomorphic fancies, which science forbids us to believe an 4 Nature compels us to employ." It is not a very lucid point when we do arrive at it. Mr. BALFOUR, to tell the truth, has , not made things quite clear to himself. He rather fears that he may have compressed his cm-Isidore- ' tions "beyond the limits of intelligibility." Many readers of the address will certainly be of that opinion. But, after all, Mr. BALFOUR'S real object was merely to throw out a few suggestions to minds more vaguely speculative than most of those which attend the meetings of the British Associa- tion. Tne view that he wished to bring out above all others, no doubt, is that which is contained in his last sentence—that as natural science grows it leans more, not less,upon an idealistic interpreta- tion of the universe, Mr. DISRAELI said at a British Association meeting many years ago : "As for me,l am on the side of the angels." S, is Mr. BALFOUR.

The Cobden Club THE CHAMBERLAIN continues to expose PROPAGANDA. the extraordinary

misstatements of Mr. CHAMBERLAIN ; but without effect SO far as concerns their withdrawal. The leading protagonist of the "raging, tearing propaganda" never, of course, made the mistake of supposing that his arguments could not be answered. Ttiey have all been answered by himself in anticinatiot. His speeches were at the best period of his life a com- plete statement of the case for Free Trade, which he considered to be impregnable. It is more the political exigencies of the day, it is probable, than a discovery of previous errors, which have led the former Secretary for the Colonies to open out his guns on he Cobdenisrn that he formerly admired and defended. Obviously he knows the nature and the fallibility of his present contentions too well to be led into defend. ing them, otherwise than by the weapon of ridicule, against those who are thoroughly acquainted with the history and effects of Free Trade. The single attempt that has been made to reply to the Cobden Club exposure, which was published some months since, has been the production of a passage from the late AUGUSTUS MOMGREDIEN, with the purpose of showing that the country was prosperous at the time of the rspeal of the C )rn Laws. If Mr. MONGREDIEN had said anything of this kind he would merely have been the less reliable historian. He would not have altered the facts, which were that the pool* were ill paid, that they were poorly employed, aiia that they wanted bread.

But though the truth as stated by the Cobden Club does not ernbarass or hinder Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN, it is, under present circumstances, of great interest and importance to the public. The latest manifesto issued by this body deals with the recent speech at Welbsck Abbey—the speech in which our neo-Protectionist promised the farm labourer 41d. a week if he would vote for Protectionist candidates. The Chamberlain contention is that the country generally is in a bad way, and that the decrease of the population in the country dis- tricts is a consequence of destitution and misery produced by Free Trade. Admitting that there are fewer men on the farms, the Cobden Club proceeds to shsw why this is and must be the case. Thera is, to begin with, the extensive use of agricultural machinery. A mowing-machine does the work of several mowers, a reaping-machine the work of a still larger num- ber of men working with sickles. Tie labourers have not remained on the farms because the work which they formerly did is now done by more rapid and economical means. But has the country suffered in consequence? Have the labourers themselves suffered The Cobden Club has no difficulty in showing ttqat the labourers have not been so much driven from the land as drawo to the towns. They have croTded into the centres of population because ia these they have found a more suffi.ling life, with higher wages, more abundant food, and a larger freedom. The exodus from the country to the towns is not a sign of national decadence,as Mr. CHAMBERLAIN pretends, but a sign, and a consequence, of national prosperity. Nothing could be further from the fact, says the Cobden Club manifesto, than Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S statement at Welbeck that the labourers were driven from the land by Free Trade, a consequerit fall in prices, and the want of employment. They left voluntarily, and with the conseqoence that there was not only an improvement in their own condition, but also in that of those who remained on the land. Farm wages rose considerably. "Both the labourers who went aud the labourers who remained had the double advantage of higher money wages and greatly reduc3d prices of food and clothing." Mr. CHAMBERLAIN says that the agricultural labourer had suffered by Cobdenistn. What is the truth? Wages have increased by 50 per cent.. since 1850, and 20 per cent. since 1870. Meantime, the essential articles of diet have fallen in price by 59 per cent. Tne labouring man is, in fact, twice as well off to-day as he was fifty years ago. "So far, then," says the Cobden Club manifesto," from Free Trade bringing disaster to labourers of any class, it has brought far cheaper food, higher money wages, and fuller employment, to a population enormously increased during the last sixty years, and it has done so to a degree which was never hoped for or predicted by its great authors." Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S next re-opening of his campaign will take place at Luton on October sth. He will then have had time to consider and digest this statement of fact; but as it does not admit of refutation he will pro- bably ignore it, according to his custom.

A new Labour party has sprung up in War- rington, i.e, the Conservative Labour party, and for which we venture to prophesy a very short existence. There is only room for one kind of

Labour party, and that is the Labour party Pore and simple. A qualified variety such as a Corm ervative Labour party won't stand. the ghost of a chance in these trouhlous tunes In fact wa are at a loss to 880 what it can find t.° do—for Labour. There may be a wild hope 111 the breasts of its promoters that it will act as. counter-blast to any Liberal Labour alliances wine' may be in the air, but they will never persuade workingmen that their aim is really the uplift.ing of labour. Why the very title of the organizatino is farcical, and when it is sponsored by the Co° servative Association openly and shamelessly the level-headed Labour man must be moved to mirth.

Mr. R Pierpoint, M.P., is again coLeerned for Cyprus. In the House of Commons on Saturdsh Sir Albert Rollia cruelly suggested that the island was a burden to this country—an aspersion which Mr. Pierpoint promptly resented, BO followed up with a dissertation on what Cyprus Is and might be. He was waxing really eloquent on his pet theme when the Speaker ruled him out 0f order. Alas! When will Mr. Pierpoiut have ail opportunity of fully airing the grievances of the distressed Cypriotes? His Parliamentary efforts) seem doomed to be nipped in the bud by sa unsympathetic Speaker. Generally, when ea M.P. fails to get a fair hearing in the 110115e' he comes home to his constituency like a child to its mother, and relates the story there. Why does not " our " member returr% to Warrington, engage Parr Hall, and te' us all about Cyprus. It would be a change frolll fiscalitis, the little loaf, and the big bung. Refer- ence books are not very generous in their deall3ga with the Mediterranean island, but sufficient cell Ibe gleaned to learn that Cyprus has had a verY chequered career. She has revolted and heel subjected innumerable times since the days Atnasis, Ptolemy and Alexander the Great, iv° all had a finger in the Cypriote pie. She has bee.n seized by the Arabs, the English and the Turks la more recent centuries, and finally handed over bY the latter to England in 1878. Since then there, have been droughts, ravages by locusts, and seve.ro Lord High Commissioners. Would Mr. Pierpoul like to spirt the latter title we wonder. If so is Warrington's boundm duty to release him fr.oca the encumbrance of Parliamentary representation, with the fervent hope that in Cyprus at any rate his w irth would be appreciated at its full value.

The Borough Treasurer has this week issued !il5 annual abstract of the accounts of the Corporatioa and the volume comprises 212 pages of closelY printed matter. It is hardly conceivable that th,l most zealous member of our Corporation 77,1' study every page, but it is always interesting know how the ratepayers' money has been 801,13..., and a good deal of information can be obtained 1" this connection by even a superficial glance at the pages of the Borough Treasurer's abstract. Pe aggregate balance sheet puts before the reader al a concrete form the financial position of thet Borough. Our indebtedness still grows and is 1.59 approaching one and a quarter millions sterling: The exact figure on the 31st of March wa. £1,208,761 15s. 2d. There are, of course, sore° valuable properties and assets to show for this expenditure, and taking the given, they they total up to £1,577,513 9s. Zr This gives a camfortable margin of over 350,vui on the right side. The four industrial concerneL the Corporation—gas works,water works,electrielki and tramway undertakings—are placed in th.: assets as being worth over a million sterling. Thi"., is a good round sum and a skilled accountSav would probably have little difficulty in showlad that the market value of the undertaKings, jedgeid by their remunerative capacity is considerebu below this estimate, and that the figures give° are altogether too round. The electricity and tranli ways undertakings are still in the experimentil.s stage, but with careful management tnere reason to believe that they will prove sound inve.su ments. When it is understood that over a redll°n; sterling is at stake, there is no reason to iraPT upon the members of the Corporation the need for the exercise of the best business qualities they 11"" endowed with.

This appears to us a very reason' outlay for what will, by the time the inquiry. is closed, be a full twelve months' investigatioo covering seven years of trading.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

PLUCKY RESCUE FROM DROWNING AT RUNCORN.

Last Saturday afternoon a number of lads were bath lag in the Ship Canal near to the Ferry Hut, Runcorn when one of them, a boy named Ashcroft, living in Waterloo road, threw up his arms and sank. When he arose he was carried out by the tide. A young man named Harry Critchley, of Egerton street. was standing talking to a man named Mannion, about 50 yards away. He saw the boy sink, and throwing off his coat he ran to the canal and entered the water. He was fortunate to overtake Ashcroft, and broughthim to the side,where after attention he seemed little worse forhis experience and was taken home. Critchley saved a life under simi- lar circumstances about seven years ago. On the present occasion he was suffering from a severe cold, a fact which enhanced his praiseworthy and plucky con- duct.

FATAL FALL AT WIDNES.

At Widnes, on Tuesday, Mr;. Mary Jane Walsh, an elderly woman residing with her married daughter in Ester-street, Widnes, died from injuries caused by fall- ing downstairs. Her son visited the house about seven o'clock in the m.rning to give his mother some money. She was in bed at the time, and got out and tapped' at the window. Whilst waiting outside he heard a noise as if someone was falling, and shortly afterwards his invalid sister opened the door. He found his mother lying at the foot of the stairs bleeding from the left ear. Dr. Schofield was quickly in attendance, but the woman died at a quarter-past nine. _

POACHLNG AFFRAY NEAR RUNCORN.

At Runcorn on Tuesday, Joseph Oakes, of Bradley Orchard,was charged with night poaching on the Aston Hall estate,near Runcorn, on Sunday. Mr.H.N.Linaker prosecuted for Mr. Hervey Talbot, J.P. William Jones, head keeper on the Aston estate, said on Sunday night he and four others were watching on land in the occu- pation of Mr. Robert Wade at Aston Grange and saw nine or ten men setting nets there. After the nets were set the men commenced driving rabbits in. The keepers then showed themselves, and the men attacked them with bludgeons, and a severe struggle ensued. The men overpowered the keepers, who were badly bruised, and made their escape carrying away the rabbits. The prisoner, who had a severe wound on the forehead, was apprehended on Monday, and he was now remanded for a week in order that the other men, whom Superinten- dent Ennion said were known,might be arrested. Prisoner applied for bail, but this was refused. There were pro- duced in court heavy broken bludgeons, which sho wed the severity of the struggle.

ST. HELENS.

Sr. HELENS AND THE EMPLOYMENT OP CHILDREN.

The St. Helens School Attendance Committee on Wednesday had before them the proposed by-laws with regard to the employment of children, and which have been the subject of great controversy and some ill-feeling in the town. The by-laws allow a child of thirteen to be employed under certain conditions, providing the child is " beneficially " employed. The Board of Educa- tion wrote enclosing the draft by-laws, and asking for certain forms to be signed. The letter also stated that the proposed definition of beneficial employment was unnecessary and must be omitted. The Town Clerk said the Board did not make any comment on the long letter the committee had written. He supposed the idea of the Board of Education was that beneficial employment meant for the child's benefit. The Chair- man (*Councillor Foote) said the definition was not in accordance with the model by-laws of the Board, and they would have their own wording. Mr. Slater said that did not affect the by-law ;it was simply a definition. The committee approved the by-laws. 'THE GLA.SS BOTTLE TRADE.—EXCEPTIONAL DEPRES-

===== 0002601_19040820_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
SION IN THE DISTRICT.

Great depression and scarcity of work at present pre- vail among the glass bottle makers of St. Helens and district. A hot summer usually produces great activity in the trade, but the heat has this year been completely discounted by the depression in trade and scarcity of money, and the glass bottle workers find themselves in the exceptional position of being put on short time in the month of August In order to prevent a large num- ber of men being stopped the men and employers have arranged a shortening of hours for the different sets of hands which has the effect of dividing up the work and spreading it over a larger number of men than would otherwise be required. Messrs. Pilkington have recently made very large extensions at their sheet glass works, the rollplate and cathedral glass sections being entirely remodelled. In connection with changes of machinery ttc. at the Ravenhead works some of the men have been out: of work, but it is believed this stoppage is only temporary. Messrs. Pilkington have erected a huge timber yard and crate-making works on the site of the old Doulton's works at Greenbank. ALLEGED RAID ON A. GROCER'S SHOP.

The circumstances attending the robbery at a grocer's shop, 45, Silkstone-street, were investigated at St. Helens Police Court, on Wednesday, when John Beddows, Thomas Kilshavv, James Woodward, and Thomas Stenton were charged on remand.—Chief Constable Wood said the men went into the lock-up shop of Patrick Conroy and took eight tins of salmon, 11b. of cheese, two loaves of bread, and a knife, value 7s. Bd. Mr. Conroy lived at 39, Borough-road, and the shop was left secure on Saturday night by Mary Murphy, manageress.— Thomas Ashall, aged fifteen, said that on Saturday night he with two other boys named Melling and Charles White, stayed out all night in order to go into the country early to catch birds. They were standing at the corner of Silkstone-street and Lawrenson-terrace when they saw the four prisoners come down the street. Kilshaw and Beddows climbed a couple of street lamps and extinguished them. They then came to him and asked for their house key in order to go .into the shop. Ashall refused it, but they got it from him under threats. They then opened the door of the shop, and Kilshaw and Beddows brought out the goods. They also brought out two bottles of toffee, but took them back. They brought out some butter and loaves, and than adjourned to some waste ground and ate the salmon and bread, giving some of it to the three lads.— Mr. W. Hutchen appeared for Stenton, and pointed out that there was really no evidence that he took any part in the robbery or handled an of the goods. Ashall was originally arrested along with the men, and he had been convicted there four times.—Charles White, aged fifteen, corroborated Ashall's story. He denied that he and his companions went into the shop when the men had left it. All the men appeared to have had some drink.—The sour men were committed for trial at the sessions.

BILLIARDS.

We have pleasure in announcing that we have made arrangements with Mr. Riso Levi, the well-known billiard expert, for a second series of weekly articles on _Billiards, to commence next month.

All strokes and positions will be treated from an ordinary amateur point of view, so that the articles will prove of great interest to the ordinary player, though strokes and positions will now and then be discussed, from which we venture to say players who occasionally make fifties and sixties may glean some- thing, and the description of the various strokes will be characterised by a high standard of thoroughness and lucidity of treatment. The first twelve articles will treat entirely of side and its effects, and the questions of transmitted side and cushion-imparted side will besone into very fully and we unhesitatingly. state that tlits chapter will prove both interesting and instructive to good class players, as well as to the ordinary player. The various strokes and positions that will be dis- cussed from time to time will be illustrated by means of

GOOD DIIGRAMS,

thus enabling players to set the balls in the positions given and practise the strokes. When any important matches or exhibition games take place, the weekly column will contain chatty notes and comments on such contests, and a list of all the principal breaks will be given. A popular feature will be the Answers to Corres- pondents," and any player submitting any question on the game may be assured of receiving a reliable answer in the Billiard column. In short, the weekly article will be a most interesting one, and will appeal to all classes of Billiard players. TEE FIRST ARTICLE WILL APPEAR ON SATURDAY SEPT. 3RD.

HANDOCK COLLIERY ACCIDENTS.

William Davies, aged twenty-five, of Cherry-street, Earlestown, was removed to the Haydock Cottage Hospital from the Princess Pit On Monday suffering from injuries to the leg. A man named Ashton, of Park- terrace, Haydnck, was also treated at the hospital after having been buried in a fall of coal in the pit, His collar bone was broken.

On Sunday the virgin peak of the Gabelhorn (Canton Valais), which has been attempted every year unsuccess- fully for the last fifty years, was climbed by M.Kern, of Geneva: M. Monod, of Paris; and the guide, Ferdin- and Furrey. Ths ascent lasted three days, the party camping twice on rocks and snow.

At Folkestone on Wednesday, Margaret Thompson, who advertised herself as "Mme. Shiela, pupil of Keiro," and as a scientific palmist and crystal gazer, was summoned for fortune telling. Detective Burniston gave evidence as to what took place ht an interview he had with the defendant. He said " Sheila " examined his hands, told him he was too generous, and that he would live to be 90. Afterwards, however, she said that at 40 he would have money left him, that he was married, and had a family. Burniston told the court that he assured "Sheila" he was single, but she said he could not deceive her. Defendant denied a portion of the detective's story, and denied fortune telling. She was a character teller, she said. She was fined 40s. or 14 days'Amprisonment, but as she pleaded that she had no money the Bench allowed her to go without pay- ment on promising to leave the town and cease practis- ing palmistry.

The Examiner, Saturday, August 2O, 1904

'RAILWAY THEFTS AT WARRINGTON, A Liking for Bottled Beer. Philip flicken, an engine driver living at 54, Dallam- lane and Herbert Ashbrook, a fireman, of 60, Leonard- street, were remanded on Saturday at the Borough Police Court on a charge of stealing from a van on the London and North-Western Railway at Dallam-lane, a 5.30 p.m. on the previous day, a case containing two dozen bottles of Bass's ale, value Gs., the property of the railway company.—The prisoners were again brought up yesterday.—Mr. W. H. Loraine of Liverooel (instructed by Mr. C. de J. Andrews) appeared to prose- cute on behalf of the company. Mr. Steel defended. I Mr. Steel said his clients were prepared to admit that they took four bottles of the ale but not the case as stated in the charge. Mr. Loraine, in stating the case for the company, saidthe cases of bottled beer wereconsigned by Messrs.- Peter Walker and Sons, brewers, to their stores in Liver pool and were handed to the company at Dallam siding. In consequence of a number of petty thefts from railway trains two detectives were put there to watch. They concealed themselves in one of the vans which was loaded with cases of bottled beer, and about 5.30 on Friday evening whilst there, they heard two men talkingand making suggestions that they could get a case of beer. Presently the door was opened, and one of the cases was taken out. The detectives emerged from the van, and found the two prisoners with thecase on the ground near the van. Hicken had four bottles of beer in his packet. They were taken to the detective office and there charged, and pleaded guilty. John Longshaw, checker in the employ of Messrs, Peter Walker and Sons, stated that he handed 350 cases of bottled ale and stout to the company's checker at Dallam Brewery on Friday last. Samuel Stewart stated that he received the consign- ment on behalf of the company. The prisoners had nothing to do with the van in which the cases were stored. Charles Hancock, a checker, said that on counting the oases, be foundthat one was missing. Richard James Owen, of the detective department, Edgehill, bore out the Counsel's statement as to con- cealing himself in the van in'company with another de- tective. He heard someone outside the van say " I think we can get one." Then another man said " I don't think so." The other man then rejoined "We will have a try." The doors of the van were opened and a case of bottled beer taken out. Witness found the case on the ground near the van and four bottle in Hicken's pockets. George Davies, detective officer at Warrington, said he charged the prisoners. Hicken said : "I have nothing else to say. lam guilty." Ashbraok said "I am guilty." Mr. Steel said the prisoners admitted taking four I bottles, but not the case. What would these men want' to take a whole case of botcled beer for? One could understand them taking one or two for a drink, but not a 'whole case. The two men were engaged during the whole of Friday last in shunting waggons along Dallam sidi g. This siding was a very difficult I one to work, and the men had been on duty from between 9 and 10 o'clock without having left the engine. They had to get their meals as they best could whilst working the siding, and seeing the beer in the waggon they were tempted and took the four bottles of beer for tocirink. Hieken, the engine driver, had been in the employ of the company for 30 years, and was a man who previously to this, had never had a bad mark against his character. He would now have to lose his situation and forfeit the benefits of the engine driver's society, amounting to something like £lOO. The fireman was 35 years of age, of previously good character and the sole support of his widowed mother. This was a ease where the men had suddenly given way to temptation and he asked the magistrates to deal leniently with them. - - -

Mr. Loraine remarked that what had been said about the men's characters was quite true, but the company had been compelled to take these proceedings on account of the large number of thefts that had taken place at this siding. The magistrates fined each defendant 40s. including costs or in default one month's imprisonment.

GENERAL BOOTH'S MOTOR CAMPAIGN.

He Arrives at Warrington To-Day.

Our readers are doubtless aware that General Booth the distinguished head of the Salvation Army, is undertaking a motor tour from St. Just (Land's End) to Ab.erdeen. This (Saturday) morning he leaves Northwich and will continue his northward journey to Warrington. He is timed to reach the Borough at 10.10 o'clock. The General is accompanied by Commissioners Nicol, Cadman Colonels Lawley, Eadie and Jeffries and several press representatives. Four cars are in attendance. Adjutant T. Everness, who is in charge of the Warrington district, will meet the General at Stockton Heath and proceed with him to the Town Hall. The route taken will be along Wilderspool Causeway, Bridge-street, Sankey-street to the Town Hall steps. The local band of the Salvation Army will meet the General at Bridge-street and head the procession to the Town Hall. It is anticipated that many local admirers of the General and his army will take the opportunity of giving him a hearty cheer and God speed on his unique tour. The Mayor (Alder- man Bolton) has expressed, a desire to personally welcome General Booth at the Town Hall and the local Free Church Council also intend to present the Salvation Army chief with an address. The cars will stop in Warrington for about ten minutes and will then proceed to Prescot where they are due to arrive at eleven o'clock. On Sunday, the General will lecture in the Hippodrome, St. Helens, on " Lessons of my Life." The Mayor (Alderman Massey) will preside. The General will also preach at night. On Monday, the tour will be recommenced and Leigh, Earlestown, Newton-le-Willows, Worsley and Man- chester districts will be visited.'

The oldest love letter in the world is in the British Museum. It is a proposal of marriage for the hand of an Egyptian princess, and it was made 3,500 years ago. It is in the form of an inscribed brick.

After a hearing lasting ill day, the magistrates of Scarborough on Thursday dismissed the case in which Norris Hepworth, jun., aged 20, son of a well- known clothier of Leeds, was charged with the man- slaughter of Lydia Unwin Death, aged 79, who died from injuries received by being knocked down by a motor-cycle, which the defendant was driving. The Chairman of the Bench said the magistrates considered it was a pure accident. POLITICS IN THE OPEN-AIR.

Tory Workinginen Support Protection.

REJOINDER BY MR. HOWES.

Bridge Foot was the scene on Thursday evening of one of the largest open-air meetings which have been held in the town for many years. The whole area from Mersey-street to the Cromwell monument was packed with a dense mass of people, aud for two hours they stood to hear political questions discussed from the standpoint of a new orgaaisationbearing the title of the Conservative Labour Party. The meeting had been arranged by the Warrington Conservatives, and four spokesmen for the new party were found in Mr. Fearn- ley (the secretary), Mr. Macartney, and Mr. Orrell, of Manchester, and Mr. Williamson, of Salford. Councillor W. J. Wr.r.soN of Latchford, presided, and in opening the proceedings saidthat meeting showed that the Conservative workingmen of Warrington had not gone entirely asleep and they meant to take more than a passing interest in the coming Parliamentary election. The main thing that concerned the working people was the terrible and increasing depression in trade, and to stop this depression should be the one effort of every man, irrespective of politics whatsoever. It could never be done by allowing the foreigners to place heavy tariffs and taxes on our goods which were being sent into their countries, and at the same time allowing them to send all their surplus and inferior goods into this country free of any tax or tariff. If they asked local firms to give them a reason for the great depression in trade, he had no doubt it would be stated that it was on account of the heavy tariffs and taxes placed upon English made goods by foreign nations, and they found it utterly impossible to make and sell their goods at a profit in foreign markets. Thus our workpeople were placed upon short time and if this were allowed to go on British work- shops would be closed to British labour. Unless some drastic reforms were made at once with regard to tariffs placed upon English goods,something very serious would happen to the working class of this country from which they would never recover. (Applause). Mr. MACARTNEV, of Manchester said there was a great deal of social discomfort at the present time and the trouble hat assumed such dimensions that the time had come for them to carefully consider their position. He advocated fair play for British labour and fair play for British labourers.

A Voice: What about Chinese labour in South Africa?

Mr. ALICARTNEY contended that there was only one political party in the House of Commons prepared to put forward definite proposals in regard to our export and import trade. The Free Trade Liberals—the free importing Liberals—were content to abide by a policy that might have been adapted to the requirements of 1846, but he urged that this policy ought to be revised now and brought up-to-date. The policy of Cobden— A VOICE : What has Cobden got to do with the present day? Mr. MA.CARTNEY : He has a great deal to do with the present day, because he enunciated a policy which the Liberal party is clinging to at the present time notwithstanding the fact that it is ill-suited to meet present day requirements. The speaker asked his hearers to protect themselves against the unfair competition of foreigners as our own markets were passing away. Foreign competitors dumped down goods here and some of the workers were inconsistent enough to believe that in some way or other this dumping promoted their interests and cheapened their food. even though it deprived them of the opportunity to work. The speaker proceeded to argue that the Conservative party had been a good friend of the workingman, and had passed the factory acts, and acts for the housing of the poor, the protection of Trades Union Funds, the Workmen's Com- pensation Act and other measures. He defied any Liberal present to point out three first class measures passed during the last 40 years by any Liberal Government for the amelioration of the lot of the workin,gclasses. In this matter of fiscal reform the Liberals were trading upon their past traditions and had not anything to bring forward. The speaker made a passing allusion to Mr. Crosfleld's speech, and on Mr. Crosfield's name being mentioned loud cheers were raised. Mr. Macartney said he was glad to see that there were some present who represented the opinions of Mr. CrosEleld because he had something to say about them. Mr. Crosfield said that the Liberal party had in Lord Rosebery the strongest possible foreign secretary alive. (Cheers.) The speaker contended that the Liberal party was a conglomeration of opposing factions. Mr. FEA.RNLEY said he was much struck in reading the speeches at the Liberal open-air meetings with the extraordinary frequency with which every speaker endeavoured to Impress upon the audience that the Liberal party was the progressive party. He com- menced to count the number of times this expression was used in the four column report that appeared in "The Examiner," but he had to give it up as it struck him that all the speeches were nothing else but the word progressive. (Laughter.) He would thank Mr. Howes if that gentleman would state any single first- class progressive measure of social industrial reform that had been passed by the Liberal party since Mr. Howes took the platform of the Liberal party and that was a long time ago. If the Liberal party had been a progressive party let Mr. Howes account for the exist- ence of the Socialist party. Was it not that the Socialists were dissatisfied with the Liberal party? A VOICE: Both of them. (Hear, hear.) . . _ .

Mr. FERNLEY: I knew you were dissatisfied with the Tory party. (Laughter.) But if the Liberal party had been a progressive party you would not have been discatistlecl with them. A VOICE : Question. Mr. FEARNLEY : it was mentioned at the Liberal meeting that we needed an alteration of the land laws. (Cheers). A VOICE : Will the Tory party do that? (Laugh- ter.) Mr. FEA.RNLEY : It was also stated that a change was needed in the House of Lords. (Cheers.) Why did not the Liberal party make these changes when they were in office? A VOICE: What was the use? Mr. FEARNLEY : That is the answer to every Liberal position. What is the use? (Laughter.) Mr. Howes told them that when the Liberals were in office they were never in power. The question naturally arose : What is the good of sending them if they cannotdo any- thing because of the House of Lords.

A VOICE: You shift them. (Hear, hear.) Mr. I'EARNLEY : We don't want them shifting. (Laughter.) They are all right. We can get them to do what we want.

A VOICE: The workingman cannot. • Mr. FEARNLEY: Yes, he can. The workingman and the Conservative party got them to pass a Workmen's Compensation Act.—(hear. hear.)—factory acts and friendly society acts. (Hear. hear.) The working classes have got more beneficial legislation from the Conservative party than the Liberal party. A Voice: Chinese labour. (Laughter and Cheers.) Mr. FEA.R.NLEY : Mr. Kier Hardie has said so. (Hear, hear.) Proceeding to explain the reasons for forming a Conservative Labour party, he said if they could bring influence to bear as a combined party upon the leachra of the Conservative party, as he believed they could, they would have the Taff Vale decision upset and the status quo which existed prior to that decision, re- turned again as definite legislation upon the statute book.

Mr. Williamson, of Salford, and Mr. Orrell, of Man- chester also addressed the meeting. They were frequently interrupted with cries of " Question " "Chinese," and "Cyprus." At the close a vote of thanks to the speakers was pro- posed by Mr. W. L. WILLIAMS. When nut to the vote there were loud cries of " No " but Mr. Williams declared the vote carried.

The bill was defeated,and Mr. Gladstone resigned. The Queen sent, for Earl Derby and asked him to undertake the forma- tion of a ministry. To the surprise of a great many people Earl Derby consented. The Tories took office without a general election and consequently, though in office, they were in a minority. Benjamin Disraili for the first time in his life was leader of the House ef Commons. The first Queen's speech introduced by this ministry contained a paragraph declaring that the Gov- ernment did not intend to touch the vexed question of Parliamentary reform. That drove the people of the country into a state of frenzy and John Bright and others started an agitation in the country. Mr. 'Disraeli then introduced a bill giving a "fancy" franchise. The Liberals knocked away clause after clause and built up a real franchise bill which was CARRIED DESPITE TETE OPPOSITION OP TILE TORTES. (cheers.) PRIMROSE LEAGUERS AT WALTON PARK.

So:ne kmasing Political Definitions.

Mr. R. I. WINNE JONES, the Ruling Councillor of the Daresbury Habitation of the Primrose League, presided at a gathering of the members and friends held in Walton Park on Saturday. in the course of an address Mr. Jones said it was li!s duty as their ruling coun- cillor to ao his best to impress upon them the urgent necessity of being particularly wide awake—in the political sense—at the present time. It needed no prophet to foretell that the country woula be asked again to record its vote at no distant period. They were aware of the attitude adopted by their mem- ber, who was a follower of Nlr. Chamberlain. The planks of Mr. Chamberlain's platform were; briefly, these : " More profit for the farmer, more employment for the labourer, and cheaper food for his family." Whether Mr. Chamberlain was,or was not,over sanguine. he was not prepared to say ; but this he did say, that under the existing deplorable state of things, and in the. absence of any other, his scheme was well worth a trial. (Hear, hear.) The Radical party, while admitting that trade, both in to.vn and country, was desperately bad, declined to giaa even a trial to any measure proposed to remedy it Instead of honestly acknowledging the unfairness and failure of so-called Free Trade, instead of suggesting a practical remedy of their own, they otill persisted in trying to blind a frequently too credulous electorate by waving before its eyes that shameful and moth-eaten old fraud—" The big Free Trade loaf." Being short of argument their opponents had taken to calling them pet names. They called them "Whole hoggers " if they were out-and-out discipins of Mr. Chamberlain, or " Half-hoggers "if they did not go quite so far as Mr. Chamberlain did with his fiscal scheme. Of course they were only speaking metaphorically, so they should be prepared to reply to them in metaphor. They remem- bered the old riddle: "Why does a donkey eat thistles ? "—the reply to which was : " Because he is an ass." (Laughter.) Well, if they were addressed by their opponents by either of those interesting names they would retort courteously, but with the confidence which is born only of conviction, that they would rather be a" whole-hogger," or even a " half-hogger," than an adjectived jackass—a jackass who preferred to starve on the thistle-tops rather than stretch his neck to taste the clover growing at his feet. (Laughter and applause).

Mr. JOHN WHITLOW proposed "That the members of the Daresbury Habitation of the Primrose League, at Walton Hall assembled, desire to record their unabated confidence in the Eton. Alan de Tatton Egerton as their representative in Parliament, and assure him that they will spare no effort to secure his re-election, when the occasion arises." Mr. Whitlow remarked that they had a very good Kin; in London, and he thought it very unwise to attempt to send another " king " there. (Laughter.) Mr. A. 1311.13N1E, Preston Brook, seconded the resolu- tion.

Mr. BOTTOMLEY, in s ipporting the resolution, said he remembered saying in Warrington recently that their Liberal friends were lying like gas meters, and he still held to that. The Government was as strong to-day and even stronger than it was at the beginning of the Session. The Liberals were saying they would "turn them out next week." They were always spotting the winners and catching losers. They were like the Russians—always being blown up with the mines they laid themselves. There might be some people present who had not yet labelled themselves. They had a right to demand that the Liberals shall de- clare.vvhat they would do if they took the place of the Conservative party. Were they going to do better? was the question, because if they got in they would not stop long. For instance, take the education question. If the Liberal party came into power to-morrow they were pledged to repeal the Education Act. Immediately they attempted that, every Irish member of Parliament —Roman Catholic—would vote against them, and they would have another election next week. If they had attempted to bring in a measure for Home Rule they would have all the Liberal party against them; if they did not attempt to bring in such a measure they would have all the Irish against them, and they would have a lot of elections. People said that elections destroyed trade and created a want of confidence in ate people, and that they were bad generally speaking, for the community at large. Therefore, they had better bear the ills they had than fly to those they knew not of. They never had a perfect GOveTnment, but he claimed the present one was as near perfection as any they had had for a long time. True, there were differences of opinion on some questions. In fact, every party was split up just now. The Government did not go as far as Mr. Chamberlain would go with regard to the fiscal question, nor as far as he (Mr. Bottornley) would go himself. What did Mr. Chamberlain want? He had declared that his policy, if carried into effect, would not inißease the price of food of either the richest or the poorest in the land, but what he did want primarily was to regain for the Government the right, in making agreements with foreign countries to defend our own, to reciprocate— to secure reciprocity and retaliation.

The resolution was unanimously passed. The CITAIRMAN said it was his duty as ruling coun- cillor to thank Sir Gilbert Greenall for so kindly allow- ing the Habitation to hold their fete at Walton Hall. (Applause). It was a difficult thing, but he must also include Lady Greenall, although she was an official of the League. They might do it in this way. They thanked Sir Gilbert also for bringing to that Habitation the most charming lady who ever presided over them. (Applause).

• The resolution was heartily passed, and cheers were given. Sir GILBERT suitably acknOwledged the resolution.

THE TRADING INQUIRY.

Three New Commissioners Appointed.

A meeting of the General Purposes Committee was held on Monday afternoon at the Town Hall. The Mayor, who presided over a large attendance of members, read the letter from Messrs. Thomas Birchall, L. Greening and A. J. Jolley, sent to him in reply to the request of the Town Council that they should go on with their duties as commissioners till the end of August. The text of the letter appeared in our last issue. As our readers will recollect the three com- missioners mentioned declined to proceed any further with the inquiry. The committee proceeded to the election of three gentlemen to take the places of Messrs. Birchall, Greening and Jolley; and the following were ultimately chosen: Councillors John Wilkinson, James Evans and , A. Peacock, who agreed to act.

It was decided ihat the Inquiry should sit three weeks longer from the date of die meeting, viz., till September sth. It was suggested that a time limit should be fixed for the drawing up of the report, but to this objections were raised, and it was not pressed, the understanding being that the report should be made as soon as possible. The first meeting of the newly-constituted commis- sion was held on Tuesday. when there was a formal ex- amination of certain books of the Corporation. There was a .further meeting on Thursday.

THE TRADES UNION CONGRESS.

. The Complete Agenda.

Yesterday the complete agenda for the Leeds Trades Union Congress was issued by the Parliamentary Committee. There are 96 resolutions on the agenda, a number larger than at any previous con- gress, but with grouping the resolutions they have been reduced to 39 sections. There are also 16 amendments, some of a vital character, to be discussed. A long discussion may arise on the questions of estab- ishing a Labour newspaper, Chinese labour, Parliamen- tary representation, the Aliens Bill, conscription, the licensing question, Trades Disputes Bill, and education laws, while the Socialist proposal for nationalising all the means of production will require a good deal of con- deration. Altogether the Congress promises to be vel y.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

THREE MONTHS FOR A RUNNING KICK.

John Brodericii,a- i;th-ollr;:, of licHaydock street, was charged on remand at the Warrington Police Court, on Saturday,before Mr. Birchall and other magistrates with having been drunk and disorderly in Turner-street, and with baying assaulted Thomas Taylor, general dealer, Turner-street.—Superintendent Monaghan stated that shortly before midnight on Sunday, August 7th, Mr. Taylor was talking to his son, when the prisoner came up and stripped himself, and said he would fight anyone in the street. Taylor asked him what was the matter, and prisoner then deliberately took a running kick at him, catching him underneath the stomach and causing him to drop to the ground. Mr. Taylor was rendered -unconscious, and had to be taken in a cab to Dr. Adams' surgery. He had suffered very great pain, and could scarcely walk even now. This was prisoner's 17th appearance. He was a notorious cornerman and had been convicted several times for theft and assault. He now pleaded guilty, and the magistrates, in sending him to gaol for the full penalty—three months' hard labour —said the penalty was sadly too light for the offence.

CAUGHT WITH HIS MOUTH TJNDER THE TAP.

At the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, before Dr. Fox and other magistrates, Wm. Campbell, 22,Lythgoes- lane, and Joseph Abrahams,6,'Dolan's-yard, were charged with having been found on the enclosed premises of Wm. Brown, licensee of the Seven Stars Inn Bridge street. on Monday, with intent to commit a felony.—Superin- tendent Monaghan informed the Bench that a butcher, named William Allen, was engaged in the Seven Stars yard, when he saw the prisoner Campbell enter the cellar. He came out and had a conversation with the other prisoner. Abrahams then went in and came out, after which both went in together. Allen reported the matter to the landlord, who on going into the cellar found Abrahams on his knees, with his month to the tap of a beer barrel, which was turned on. Campbell was hiding behind the door in a drunken condition. P.C. Phillips was acquainted with the facts and took Abrahams into custody.—Campbell who was very drunk followed him, and said, Joe. I'm as bad as you.' When the constable had seen Abrahams safely in the Police Station he went outside and brought Campbell in.—ln describing Abrahams, the superintendent sail he was a man who did anything and carried parcels if they were not too heavy.—Prisoners were sent to gaol for seven days.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Ebenezer Church.

There was a large congregation in Ebenezer Church on Sunday, when the Rev. Alfred Barratt preached from 1 Sam. xx. 3: "A step between me and death." The Christian, he said, never died, but simply crossed the borderland into eternal glory. For making that one step he urged them to prepare themsalves by thinking of their ways. The rev, gentleman made sympathetic allusion to the deaths of three children of members of the congregation.

===== 0002601_19040820_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
Musical Service at St. Peter's.

An "open" meeting of the men's servise at St. Peter's Church was held on Sunday. There was a large congre- gation. Miss Bessie Eckersley, who possesses a good soprano voice, sang with much taste, as did also Mr. Levi Richardson, tenor. Master Tom Barton, a young violinist of mach promise, highly pleased the congrega- tion with his violin solos, while the boy organist,Master Willie Pierce, played surprisingly well. At the close of the service, the Rev. D. R. James, curate, thanked the artistes for giving them such a treat. Mr. F. Webster was the organist and accompanist. The collections amounted to £l. 9s. 6d., an. increase of 4s. upon last year's amount.

After speaking of Christ's life and character, the preacher asked the question, could we accept such a lofty character as our example and bring that life into relationship with our own. In answering it he pointed out that Christ had led the way and had triumphed over sin in order that others also might be victors. The Rev. J. Davies and "Duty."

There was a well-attended open meeting of the War- rington P.S.A. Society in the. Co-operative Hall,Sankey- street, on Sunday. The Chairman was Mr. H. Taylor (police court missionary), who spoke On" The Pleasant side of Christianity." He urged all to get men into the P.S.A., and help to make more happy homes in Warring- ton.—Miss Hill rendered two solos very nicely and was accompanied on the piano by Mr. Wadsworth.—The Rev. John Davies gave an address on" Duty." He re- minded his hearers of the famous utterance of Nelson, when he said "England expects that every man this day will do his duty." Not only was the sailor and the soldier to do this, and the collier who risked his life under the surface, but everyone. Nobody was sent into this world without a purp me,and unless they all worked for the uplifting of their fellow men they were not doing their duty. True enough there was a great deal of wickedness in the world,but there were so many earnest men in the world trying to do their duty that there was no fear of the Devil capturing all. If all parents would bring Christ more into the home-life, and let their chil- dren have a good influence imparted into them, it would be a grand thing for Christianity.

HUMAN LIVES TEN SHILLINGS

EACH.

Ten shillings ! It seems a low price for the life of any man or woman doesn't it?

- Has anybody ever dared to estimate human life at so cheap a rate? Let us turn the pages of history and see. Tn 1802 the British Parliament granted Dr. Edward .Tenner £10,090, and in 1807 £20,000 more, as a reward for his discovery of viccination. When the second bill was under discussion, Admiral Berkeley stated that there was evidence to show that vaccination had saved 40,000 lives in great Britain alone; and that, assuming these lives to be worth ten shillings apiece, the doctor was entitled to £20,000. _ _

Thanks to Dr. Jenner. we have had the whip hand of smallpox for more than ,a century. But is it so with other diseases? Statistics prove that not less than a fifth of our people constantly. suffer from dyspepsia. For ages it has, been the despair of medical men. An eminent physician not long ago wrote thus to a pro- fessional friend: "I would rather see a patient with almost any other disease enter my consulting room than one afflicted with the seven devils of indigestion and dyspepsia." Yet it is about this very disease that thousands ot people write thus: "I cannot express how grateful I am for the good Mother Seigel's Syrup has done me. I don't know where I should have been without it." This is from a statement made by Mrs.D. Bunyan,of 35,Moness- street, Abbott-road, Poplar, E., on Bth April, 1904. Mrs. Bunyan continues: ""[used to suffer terribly from in- digestion. My appetite was poor, and I nearly always had a pain at the chest, which food made worse. Sometimes I would have such violent headaches that If would become dizzy and almost blind. It was impossible for me to eat anything, however 'Ugh- without it producing a feeling of fulness and oppres sion. All energy seemed to leave me, and when not in pain I could have fallen asleep at any moment. This continued for months, until I was quite ,laid up. I tried many medicines without benefit before I thought of Mother Seigel's Syrup. When at length I did so, that was the beginning of my cure and the end of my trouble. The pain ceased as if by magic, and I improved daily. In a week or so I was about again feeling brighter and stronger than ever before. .My health is now excel- lent.' In its own way Mother Seigel's Syrup may fairly claim to do a service as valuable to mankind as Dr. Jenner's discovery. It promotes health, and health is the foundation of wealth.

•There'are bad times ahead for the soap-boilers, to judge from an account given in t!. Kansas paper of a, mountain at Elko, in Nevada, which contains an inex- haustible supply of pure soap. Anyone, we are told, may enter the mine with a butchers knife and cut as large a piece as he wants. It is beautifully mottled,and on being exposed to the air hardens somewhat. The mountain of clay is of fine texture, and•it contains boracic acid, soda, and borate of. lime. Its colour is given it by the iron and other minerals. In its natural state it is rather strong in Alkali, and removes ink and other stains readily. At one tune it was used in all the lavatories of the Pullman cars, but as soon as the fact became generally known the cakes were carried away by travellers as souvenirs. The railway could not supply the demand, so it was forced to discontinue its use.

PUBLIC BENEFIT. UNDER the CLOCK for BEST BOCT in LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Death of Mr. John Wilding. Many will hear with regret of the death of Mr. John Wilding, furniture dealer, of Buttermarket-street and Winwick street, who passed away on Sunday evening. The cause of death was syncope. He was out of doors for the last time on Thursday, the 4th inst., and there was then no indication that the end was so near. Three weeks ago he returned from a visit to the Isle of Man and seemed to have benefited by the change. His complaint, however, became much worse a week prior to his death. He was 61 years of age. He had spent the greater part of his lifetime in Warrington and by business aptitude he built up a good connec- tion and established himself as a successful trades- man. The funeral took place in the • Cemetery on Thursday afternoon. LADIES BEST GLACE BOOTS and SHOES, UNDER the CLOCK. BRID GE-STREET.—Ad7+- School Children's Treat. Mr. W. C. Jones, of Llannerch Park, St. Asaph, and late of the Oaklands, Preston Brook, kindly gave the day and Sunday sehool children at Daresbury, MoorA, Hatton and Preston Brook a treat on Friday last, in commemoration of the coming of age of his son Mr. Piers Jones. About 350 children assembled in a field kindly .placed at their disposal by Mr. Billington, of the Village Farm, where swing boats, cocoanut stalls, Aunt Sallies, donkeys, &c., were provided for their free use until dark. An excellent tea was prepared , by teachers and friends from the different schools, and partaken of in the Daresbury Council School at four o'clock, after which sports of all kinds for useful presents, were eagerly indulged in until dusk, when the customary cake and sweets terminated a most enjoyable afternoon.

===== 0002601_19040820_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR STOTT AND THE

MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT. --- Criticism of Town Council Apathy.

On behalf of the Independent Labour Party, a large open air meeting was addressed by Councillor Stott last Sunday evening at Bridge Foot. His subject was the Medical Officer's report and its lessons. He re- marked that attempts were made to get a full discussion on the report at the last meeting of the Town Council but without success. Two hours were spent on the Non-Trading Inquiry question, or rather be would say on personalbickering ; but on such an important ques- tion as the health of the town the Council could not afford time for discussion. We were paying £5OO a year .to our Medical Officer and when his annual report is presented, containing valuable suggestions for the im- provement of the health of the town, those suggestions were apparently treated with contempt by many members of the Council. Surely the health of the citizens is of sufficient importance to receive the serious considera- tion of our public representatives. The death rate for the Borough has more than once during recent months been the highest in the country. One week it was as high As 25 in the 1000, and the lowest death fate for the same week for the large towns was 7 per 1000. The high death rate of Warrington was commented upon by many of the leading London and' ' provincial papers. Members of the Town Coun- cil object to Warrington being "blackballed.' ; He, Councillor Stott, had no desire to blackball our I Borough, but the published figures condemned us,and it would be well for us to recognise our true position. Last year the deaths of children under five years of age were nearly half the total deaths of the town. The figures were, for deaths, over five years of age 635, under five years 602. Even though the deaths of adults last year were lower than the average, yet the death rate for all ages was 2 per 1,000 more than the average for the large towns. Last year there were 100 deaths from con- sumption,a disease which is stated by the highest medi- cal authority to be a preventible disease. Councillor Stott read extracts from the report in reference to the condition of the elementary schools, which are seriously overcrowded, imperfectly ventilated, and wretchedly lighted, and conduce to the spread of infection. With the adoption of the new Education Act, we in Warring- ton had unfortunately to take over the control of many schools which were very unsatisfactory from a health standpoint. Many of them were also under-staffed with teachers and caretakers badly paid. In fact we may safely say that some of the schools through lack of funds were worked under a kind of sweating system. Two years ago the Board of Education threatened us with a School Board if steps were not taken to provide additional school accommodation. After sucha length of time, little if anything seems apparently to have been done to provide the necessary accommoda- tion. The speaker suggested that a medical officer should be appointed for the schools, whose duty should be to visit the schools daily and examine any child who appears to be unwell, or who complains of illness. If any are found to be suffering from disease the homes of the children should be visited so that investigations may be made to find out the cause of the disease. Much of the expenditure on elementary education was to a great extent wasted in trying to teach children who were hungry, and who were often thought to be stupid. Steps ought to be taken to make provision for;feeding those children who go hungry to school, and it is a fact that during the last ewelve months many children have gone to school in a morning insufficiently fed. Until additional school accommoda- tion was provided, parents should be discouraged as far as possible from sending children to school who were under five years of age. These reforms would of course i cost money, but n his opinion the health and comfort of the children should be the first consideration. He believed that many of the epidemics might be pre- vented, which would mean decreased hospital expenses, an increased school attendance and consequently a larger Government grant. The question of unpaved streets and back passages had been commented on by the press and from the pulpit, and was a most serious question. Councillor Stott read extracts from the Medical Officer's report in reference to the number of unpaved streets and passages, with which he entirely agreed. Several of the worst streets and passages were referred to by the speaker. West-street in Orford Ward in particular and he criticised the excuses male in the Town Council for %Vest-street not having been psved. For members of the Council to say there were difficulties in the way and that money must first be borrowed before the street could be paved were not sufficient reasons for its still being unpaved, when we find it has been unpaved for nearly 20 years. The property owners were shown too much consideration, and many of them were not deserving of any such consideration. He was in entire agreement with Dr. Gornall that the passing of plans for new property should be madea con- dition that the streets should be formed and paved. Councillor Stott's criticisims on the unpaved back passages were mast scathing. Owing to them being unpaved they were not swept. They tended to encourage dirtiness and filth. In some instances fowls were kept. All kinds of garbage were thrown out and left there to rot. He had seen children paddling in excreta which had accidentally spilled from the pails,or had come from leaking pails. Very many of the ash places were with- out any fasteners, some of them with broken hinges. He had seen in one back pass tge not less than 30 per cent. of these doors open on one particular occasion. The result was that young children could frequently be seen playing in the ash places, stirring up the refuse in the ashbins, and with their childish inquisitiveness peering into the closet pails. Surely that was not a 1 desirable state of things, especially at a time when fever I was so prevalent. rho speaker also referred to the slum dwellings which there are in some parts of the town. Many of these hovels were not fit for human habitation in fact no man would house his cattle in them if he valued them.CouncillorStott suggested that the only real remedy for the housing question was to demolish these wretched buildings which wee° a standing menace to the town and to erect good sound municipal dwellings for the working classes. The provision of a sterilised milk depot was then recommended. They had been tried in many towns and in almost every instance had proved to be beneficial in decreasing the infantile death rate. The cases of diarrhcea amongst infants fed on cow's milk had this year been very numerous. The Medical Officer had told us what ought to be done and if his sugges- tions are not going to be noticed, then in his, (the speaker's,' opinion, those committees responsible for carrying out the work were deserving of the strongest -condemnation. It was the children of the working classes who were over-nrowded in our elementary schools and had to play in our unpaved streets; it was the workingman and his family who had to live in the slums. The children of the workers were killed off at a much more rapid rate than the children of the upper middle classes. The workingman possessed the large majority of the votes and he trusted that those votes would be used to return men to the Town Council who would strive to imProve the conditions under which they had to work and live. If the workers showed they were determined that the seggestions of our Medical Officer shall be carried out then the Town Council dare not neglect any longer.

===== 0002601_19040820_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
A WARRINGTON WOMAN'S SAD END. -- .Found Drowned in the Mersey.

The body of Bridget Sankey, 53 years of age, wife of John Sankey, a labourer in the employ of the Man- chester Ship Canal Company, residing at 32, Hind-street, was recovered from the Versey on Sunday near Howley Quay. The circumstances attending her death were inquired into by Mr. Brighonse, county coroner, on Tuesday even;ng, and they were of a distressing charac- ter.—The woman's husband said his wife was addicted to drink, and that during the past'month she had been drinking rather heavily. He last saw her alive at 12.30 on Saturday afternoon, when he went home to dinner. She then appeared to be sober. Hs returned to work and remained there until 5.30. He then went to the house of his married 'daughter in Fairclough's-avenue and arrived home at 11.30, quite sober. His wife was not there, and as he could not see her about he and his two daughters made a search until early in the morning. Eventually they thought she hadigone into a neighbour's house and gone to sleep, and so they went to bed. As she did not return, home on the Sunday, information was given to the police. In the afternoon he heard that a body had been recovered from the river, and sub- sequently he identifled,it as that of his wife. He did not give his wife his wages on Saturday as she would have most probably spent them in drink, but gave them to his eldest daughter, who resides with him. His wife had often gone down to where he worked to ask him for the price of a drink,and he thought probably she had been going to see him on the Saturday night. She had taken two sheets from off the beds in his house, without anyone knowing, and got 26. for them at a pawnshop.— Joseph Rimmer, assistant to Messrs. J. H. and H. M. Jackson, pawnbroker, Caurch-street, said Mrs. Sankey came into the shop at about nine o'clock on Saturday night when she offered for pledge two sheets. She had had drink, but he thought she was quite capable of taking care of herself and gave her 2s. He did not see in which direction she went.—Thomas Etherington. captain of the boat Naseby employed by the Manches- ter Ship Canal Company, and residing at 13, Melbourne- street, said that at two o'clock on si he Raw the body floating at Howley stirnedaet, y Hulme, afternoon Quay. The tide was then coming in. He got a boat hook and brought it to the side. He then informed the police who took the bodyaway.---Acting Coroner Officer Dennett said a dirt and one boot were missing from the body, but that was probably accounted for by pass- ing boats.—The Jury returned a verdict of" Found drowned."

STOCKTON HEATH GARDEN SOCIETY. ____ Third Annual Exhibition.

I The Church Institute at Stockton Heath was on ' Saturday the scene for the third time of the annual , exhibition of flowers plants and vegetables organised by the Stockton Heath Garden Society. The main hall I was lined with tables on which a choice collection of ' flowers and plants was exhibited, whilst the smaller room behind the platform was utilised for the exhibi- tion of vegetables. The show was a distinctly creditable one, and there are evidences in the village that the society has already done flinch to encourage horticulture by creating a healthy rivalry for the prizes offered. This is especially noticeable in the cottage gardens, many of them being perfect pictures with their sweet scented roses and clingingclematis. The number of entries for the show was 10') in excess of last year, but owing to the dry summer, the exhibits were fewer and hardly up to the quality of the flowers shown last year. The vegetables were ketter both as regarded quantity and quality, and hasoffie of the classes there was keen competition for the prizes. The respon- : sible work of judging was performed by the following: Outside competitions, Mr. Parker (Warrington park • keeper) and Mr. Parkinson (gardener at Appleton Hall); ; inside competitions, Mr. J. Lockhart (Walton Hall) and Mr. E. Slater (Norton Priory) ; table decorations, Mrs. T. H. Lyon and Mrs. Vere B. Davies. The flower exhibits chiefly consisted of asters, dahlias, pansies and sweet peas, and additional beauty was lent to the scene by the many groups of flowers and plants sent for exhibition only, these including blooms from , the grounds of Mr. and Mrs. T. H. Lyon, Mr. and Mrs. I Vero B. Davies, the Mayor (Alderman Bolton), Mr. Moore, Mr. E. Vernon, Mr. Leonard Davies, and Mr. G. Tandy,_

Mr'. Vero B. Davies is president of the society, but an unfortunate attack of illness prevented him from being present at the opening ceremony. A meed of praise is due to Mr. T. R.Westbrook (hon treasurer),Mr. F. R. Temperley (hon secretary) and the committee for the excellent arrangements which had been made. THE OPENING CEREMONY.

The Rev. H. V. Pigot presided at the opening cere- mony. He referred to the absence of the President,Mr. Vere B. Davies through illness, and said doubtless his recovery ha fil been retarded by the unfortunate occur- rence which had aroused the sympathies of the whole village, Mr. Davies was a good friend to the Garden Society, and they missed his presence very much. He would now call upon Mrs. J. C. Parr to declare the show open. _ _ _ _

Mrs. Parr said: It gives me the greatest pleasure to be here this afternoon, and lam delighted to see such an excellent display of plants and flowers. There is no more satisfactory recreation than gardening. It is a never-ending interest and pleasure, with always some- thing new to learn. We all appreciate the pretty gardens of Stockton Heath, and I should like to say that many people have especially admired the roses and clematis which grow so well on houses not far from here. This society deserves much congratulation and every en couragement, and lam very glad to have the oppor tunity of opening their show and wishinglthem success- (Applause). Mrs. Parr then presented a number of special prizes. Mr. Plinston proposed a vote of thanks to the judges, and said he thought it only right that the gentle- men who filled such responsible positions should have their work recognized. He had had a look round the room, and could say that all the awards had been made on pure merit and that the judging had been no light task.

Mr. Pearson seconded, and the motion was heartily carried.

Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Parr for her kindness in opening the show. He was very pleased, he said, to hear her sensible remarks about the practical usefulness of a show of 'that kind, and, he hoped the words which fell from her lips would be borne in remembrance and that next year the exhibi- tion would show an increase both in merit and use- fulness.

Mr. Broady seconded and the motion was cordially agreed to.

-On the motion of Mr. Pearson, the Vicar was thanked for2pres!ding.

The Vicar said he felt that thanks were due to those responsible for fhe establishing of the society, who had every reason to congratulate themselves. Stockton Heath wal.. progressing by leaps and bounds, and they wished to maintain its reputation of being the prettiest suburb of Warrington. (Hear, hear.) He thought the Garden Society were doing a great deal to help to maintain that reputation. (Applause). THE PRIZE LIST.

MOTOR NOTES.

There are some things that are better managed in Chicago than in England. In that enlightened city a Board of Registry has just been established with a view of regulating the issue of diving licences only to those persons who are fit, mentally and physically, to receive them. Intoxicated and reckless persons are debarred from registration, and all applicants must demonstrate that they are perfectly capable of driving and control- ling a car before the licence can be granted them. In England, the payment of the fee of five shillings ensures a licence being granted to anyone who has passed the specified age limit, and the halt, the lame, and the blind all are eligible, as long as they can pay the sum required.

What to do with a slipping clutch is sometimes per- plexing, especially to the comparative novice who may be touring,when the defect first becomes noticeable,sum miles from the reach of expert aid. The first thing to do is to ascertain the cause of the slipping. If it is due to wear of the leather or fibre face,a little powdered resin may be used. To prevent the grip being too fierce it is wise to mix French chalk with it in about equal proportions. Let the clutch be withdrawn and this mixture inserted between the two surfaces.

Should the slipping, however, be found to be due to oil on the leather, resin must not be employed, but plain chalk, or preferably Fuller's earth, should be used instead.

When the slipping is due to a badly worn leather and there seems to be little hope of mending matters by the plan suggested,some advocate the desperate plan of blocking the clutch. This is usually done by inserting bits of cotton waste between the two portions of the clutch so that it becomes wedged in position and cannot be withdrawn. But a little reflection will show that this is indeed a desperate and a dangerous course to take. In the first place, it will be impossible to change the gear, since the clutch cannot be withdrawn, and the driver, starting presumably on his lowest in order to ascend any hill, will run some risk of over-heating the motor. In the second place, it will be extremely danger- ous in traffic or in the event of having to pull up suddenly—and this latter contingency may occur even in a comparative wilderness. It is true that the brakes will be available; but, putting aside the mechanical iniquity of using these with the engine still connected,it is possible that the engine will overrun the car, and in that case there is nothing but disaster to be faced.

When a clutch is found to be slipping badly from excessive wear, the best plan is to use the resin, omit- ting the chalk from the mixture, and to continue to 65 this as the effects weir off, driving carefully whilst she is running towards the nearest garage where a new leather can be obtained and fitted. The progress may possibly be very slow, but it has the advantage of not injuring the motor, and, with patience, the goal will eventually be reached.

As a rule, side-slipping is a thing to be avoided, but there are times, strange as it may seem, when a little skid is very useful. Such an occasio% arises when it is necessary to turn a car designed with a long wheel base in a narrow road. The new 12 h.p. Decauville, for instance, has a longer wheel-base than the older pattern, and is indeed for its h.-p. about the longest on the market. This is, of course, a mechanical advantage, the only drawback being the trouble that a turn in a lane necessitates. However, by a little judicious skidding it is quite as easy to turn the new 12 h.p. Decauville in a narrow road as the old 10 used to be. To acquire the accomplishment of side-slipping in the nick of time is useful for anyone who drives a car with a long-wheel base and is given to rambling in such narrow lanes as abound in parts of Hertfordshire and Essex.
