KILLED WHILST AT PLAY. A Penketh Sad End.

A painful sensation WAS caused in Penketh on S Itnr- day afternoon when it became known that Hilda Unsworth Wood, the six-year-old daughter of Mr. David Wood, of Bold-view, Sankey-road, Penketh, had been killed whilst at play. A sad feature of the case was that the girl's parents had only departed that morning for Southport to spend their hllidays, their little daughter and her sister having accompanied them to the station to see them off. A telegram was immediately despatched to Mr.and Mrs. Wood asking them to return at once. On their arrival at Sankey Station, the sad news was broken to them by the Rev. John Gunnell. —The circumstances attending the death of the girl were inquired into by Mr. Brighouse and a jury, at the Sankey Chapel House on Tuesday evening. Mr. David Wood, father of the girl, was the first witness called. His daughter, he said, was six years of age, and he last saw her alive on the morniagof the accident when he departed for Southport. He did not attach the sliuhtest blame to anyone.

Mr. F.R. Roberts, who appeared for Messrs. H. and J. Heesom, the builders of the property where the accident happened, expressed the deep sympathy of the flrm with the bereaved parents. Mr. Heesom was terribly upset about the matter. He (Mr. Roberts) had personally known Mr. Wood all his life, and would like to add his own sympathy with Mr. Wood and his wife in the sad occurrence.

Harry Gibson Wood, brother of the deceased girl, related the circumstances attending the accident. About two o'clock on Saturday afternoon they were playing in front of some houses in course of erection close to their own home. His sister and another girl had been playing on a temporary bench. Her com- panion was called away and his sister was getting off the bench when it came with her and knocked her down. A loose beam fell off the bench and caught her face and the bench fell on her legs. William Rigby, of Halton View, Widnes, stated that he was attracted to the scene by screams. On arrival he found the child's head under a beam and a joiner's bench across her legs. He released her and she was taken home by her sister. The accident took place on private property. Mr. Roberts said his client had sent hundreds of chil- dren away. The Coroner said the question for the jury was a simple one, though the circumstances of the child's death were very sad. The property, according to a witness, was fenced in, but they all knew what children were, and how they got into places to play. One might think that holding so many inquiries day after day he would get hard hearted on such matters, but he did not because as a father he knewjust what p. father's feelings were. Some parents, in their grief, said things they ought not to say, but which they could not help saying owing to the state of their feelings. Mr. Wood, how- ever said frankly that there was not the slightest blame attaching to anyone, and there was no doubt that was so. They all sympathized with him in his trouble. He would like to say that he (the Coroner) very much appreciated the fact of Messrs. Heesom being officially represented, as it showed respect and feeling. The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death," and expressed their sympathy with the parents. They did not attach blame to anyone.

MR. RIDER HAGGARD'S DOG AND OTHER OMENS.

Mr. Rider Haggard's recent dream that his dog was drowning and its fulfilment, has caused a " Truth 'con- tributor to revive a number of old stories of omens. Among these is the story of Vandyke, who, having drawn King Charles I. in three different faces, a profile- three-quarters, and a full-face, sent the picture to Rome for Bernini *A3 make a bust from it. Bernini was unaccountably dilatory in the work; and, upon this being complained of, he said that he had set about it several times, but there was something so unfortunate in the features of the face. that he was shocked every time he examined it, and forced to leave off wprk, observing that, if any stress was to be laid on physiog- nomy, he was sure the person whom the picture repre- sented, was destined to a violent end.

The bust was, at last. finished, and sent to England. As soon as the ship that brought it arrived in the Thames the King, who was very impatient to see the bust, ordered it to betaken immediately to Chelsea. It was accordingly carried thither, and placed upon a table in the garden, whither the King went, with a train of nobility, to inspect the work. As they were viewing it, a hawk flew over their heads, with a partridge in his claws, which he had wounded to death. Some of the partridge's blood fell upon the neck of the bust, where it remained without being wiped off. This bust was placed over the door of the King's closet at Whitehall and remained there till the palace was destroyed by fire.

When Georee 111. was crowned, a large emerald fell from his crown; America was lost in his reign. When Charles X. was crowned at Rheims, he accidentally dropped his hat'; the Due d'Orl4ans (Louis Philippe) picked it up and presented it to him. On the Saturday preceding the promulgation of the celebrated ordon- nances by Charles X.'s ministers, the white flag being hoisted on the column in the Place Vendome, and always hoisted when the Royal Family was in Paris, was observed to be torn in three places: the tricolour waved in its stead the following week. On the morn- ing of the rejection' by the House of Lords, of the first Reform Bill," the dawn" was, indeed, overcast with storm.

THE "WHITE KING'S" MISFORTUNE FORESHADOWED. At the period of Napoleon's dissolution, on the 4th of the month in -which he expired, the island of St. Helena was swept by a tremendous storm, which tore up almost all the trees about Longwood by the roots. The sth was another day of tempest, aud at about six in the evening, Napoleon muttered " Tete d'arm4e "and expired. Charles I. refused to wear at his Coronation the robes of Edward the Confessor which all his predecessors had worn, but was dressed in white velvet. Lord Pembroke entreated him not to use that costume, for the mis- fortunes of "the White King" had long been predicted! A sudden storm tore the Royal Standard on the Tower of London at the outset of the Stuart dynasty. Of James 11. there is this omen, amongst many others : "The Crown, being too little for the King's head, was often in a tottering condition, and like to fall off." The same day, the King's Arms, pompously painted in the great altar window of a London church, suddenly fell down without apparent cause, and broke to pieces, whilst the rest of the window remained fixed."

THE MOON.

Viewed through all her phases, from the thin crescen to the fully developed Orb, the moon is tbe largest! most beautiful and interesting object in our nocturnal sky. And from the nights in Eden when Adam first' surveyed the wonderful Lunar phenomena until now, men in all times, and in all degrees of civilisation, hito sought to understand and explain her mysteries. ?" as might have been expected, the result has been (115- appointing, little more than theories having bll, brought to light. • A few facts, however, which, a from its smaller volume, distinguish this great Lnallur ary from the Sun may be mentioned. The light of the Sun which is bright and dazzling, is of a reddish bile' and when its rays are focussed through a burning-glass' they will burn wood, and even fuse metals. Oa thl other hand, the Moon's light is pale and silvery, when treated thronei the lens is so cold that it Wl' not sensibly affect the most, delicate thermometer: Again, the sunlight dries and preserves certain kinds ot fish and fruit; whereas moonlight turns such to putrefaction. Once more, sunlight will often put a coal fire: but such a fire seldom burns brighter than when the Moon is shining upon it. One finds it itaPs. sible to reconcile these simple facts with the Astron,.°; mical teaching that the Moon is not a" Gnat Light, as the Bible says it is, but only reflects the light of We Sun. Hail, lovely Orb, so mild, serene, " That shin'st in thine own virgin ray& ; Thou glory of the nightly scene, Upon thy face we love to gaze ! The stars that in thy pathway shine Thy more resplendent glory own; 13ow to thy precedence divine, And slowly fade before thy throne. In thy pure light Heaven nearer seems, To waiting souls with quickened eyes; And this wide vale a land of dreams, Where only peaceful visions rise. In every land, in every age, Thy charms, that nothing can alloy, Have been to savage and to sage, Unfailing wells of purest joy. And men still love thy gentle reign, That ever com'st the world to bless, With boundless stores of fruit and grain, And Autumn's tender loveliness. 0, then let men adore the Hand That placed the Moonlight in the sky; A priceless boon to sea and land, A joy for every human eye. J. H. W. CLINDINNING.

THE OPAL RING.

EY SHIRLEY RAYNARD, Author of "A Widow's Love Story," "Under the Holly," "Robert Lanyon's Mistake," &c.

It was a very brilliant opal, in an old-fashioned, -curious gold setting. Lucy Gresham slipped the ring on to the third finger of her left hand, and examined it carefully. The light from the candle, which stood upon her bureau, played upon the stone, chewing up the beautiful tints and fire with;.' • She then took up the case in which the rilig -nad been enclosed, and found in the back of grk lid a small piece of notepaper. This she unfold' With trembling fingers, for she saw that the hailers srriting upon it was that of her mother. "My dear Lucy," it ran. "When you read these words I shall be no more. It is my Particular wish that after my death you should always wear the enclosed ring. It has been in talY possession for many years, and, though you 'lave never seen me wear it, I have always looked uPon it as one of my greatest treasures. Many Memories are linked with it, which I am almost te, mpted to disclose, but perhaps it is for your u.rappiness that I should refrain from so doing. enclose it for the last time in its case, know 21 that you will find it when you look throt ga 1313' papers in the old bureau.—Your ever loving tnother, MARY GRESHAM." What could it mean ? she wondered. What Were the memories linked with the ring? She Carefully replaced the little box, blew out' the candle, and sat down in a low chair by the fire. She leant back dreaming of the past, and of the. future before her. Life seemed so strange now that she was left entirely alone, her mother gone ; she who had been the mainstay of her own existence. Fortunately the cares of poverty did ant form part of her musings, for she was provided With a very comfortable income. It was now five weeks since Mrs. Gresham had Passed gently away from life, and Lucy way becoming more used to her loneliness. She wag beginning to depend more upon herself. Necessity Obliges us all to use our limbs, or fall to the ground when our props are taken away. Lucy found that she must now think for herself, and act upon her own decisions. She felt wonderfully free to act as she liked—too free, for she had not a tie lu the world—no one upon whom to pour forth her vast stores of love and sympathy. At twenty-three she was her own mistress, so far as fate will allow auy of us truthfully to use that phrase. In one of the many conversations with her mother during her last illness, Mrs. Gresham had urged Lucy to travel when she should find herself alone. Her Words came back now to her memory, and Lucy resolved to :nake preparations for leaving home at ?ace. The much-loved old home had become a burden to her, filled as it was with ghostly Memories. She decided to spend some time on the Continent of Europe, and as autumn advanced cross the Atlantic and see something of America. The first of June saw Lucy aboard the Channel steamer. She was accompanied by Mrs. Bruce, a distant relation, who was only too glad to be able lo see something of the world at no expense to nerself. She was a good-tempered, pleasant wontitn, with no very pronounced opinions, nor anY great will-power of her own. Months passed away as they wandered from Place to place, staying a shorter or longer time in each as inclination directed. Early in September they found themselves in Paris, where Lucy determined to stay a few days before returning ts ogland en route for America. Paris was delightful in the autumn sunshine. The air was crisp and invigorating, and the girl felt in altogether better spirits than she had done since her mother's death. Health had given colour to her pale cheeks and roundness to her tall, slender figure. She and Mrs. Bruce. thoroughly enjoyed their few days' shopping. _ _ _ _

one arti;on, returning to the hotel after a long drive in the Bois, they walked through the hall, in which several people were standing. As they passed the office Lucy paused to receive her letters. A gentleman was also waiting for his. As he stood he rested his left hand Upon the ledge of, the window, and Lucy noticed the flash of an opal. During the few moments i!he waited her eyes were riveted to the man's ring, 'or she saw that in every particular it exactly a:latched her own. Had the rings been of ordinary Workmanship she would have, thought nothing 4t all of the coincidence, but such was no the ewe. The rings were most curious, both the setting and shape of the opals, and in the enzraxing of the gold. Having obtained her letters, she and Mrs. Bruce Passed on and sat down to dinner. "Did you observe that man who was waiting for his letters?" said Lucy.

"I noticed there was a man, and that he did /lot look like a Frenchman," returned Mrs. Bruce.

"He is wearing a ring exactly like mine," said Licy, turning her own round upon her slender anger. _

You must be mistaken," returned Mrs. Bruce. "There could not be another so curiously made as Yours. It is evidently an antique." "That makes it all the more strange," remarked Lucy. "I was quite close to him, and I had time tO notice everything about it." "Well, if so, it is a very strange thing," said Mrs. Bruce, doubtfully. And so the subject tlrflppe4l._ . _ .

From Paris they returned to London, staying a few days to attend to matters of business before crossing to America. Lucy's house being let furnished, she put up at an hotel, where she found a packet of letters awaiting her. One of them was from the present tenant of her house, Whom she knew slightly. Mrs. Harbury wrote to may that. a gentleman had called to se* Lucy some days before. He had given the name of Remble, and said he had come over from America, and that he had most important business with Miss Gresham. He had seemed very disappointed When told that she was out of England, and had made particular inquiries as to where she Was to be found. ."Of course, I could not give him much information beyond a general outline of Your route. I told him about what date yo" expected to sail for America," the letter concluded

"Strange!" exclaimed Lucy, as she folded tt.* letter. "And from America, too. My fate seem, linked in some way with that country. I suppose the mystery will solve itself in some way if I have patience—most mysteries do." Business despatched in London, Lucy and her companion sailed from Liverpool by Cunard boat, bound for New York. The weather was perfect. Never did the Atlantic shew more consideration, and Lucy, who was a good sailor, enjoyed the voyage from the first. Even Mrs. Bruce started well, and came bravely to her seat for .the first few meals. Hardly were they settled in their Places for dinner when Lucy observed the man or the opal ring walk into the saloon, and, as luck NvoUld have it, he took his place next to Mrs. Bruce. Lucy recognised him at once, and wanted to explain to Mrs. Bruce that he was the man she had seen in Paris, but she was afraid of being overheard, and so waited with what patience she ocMld until the meal should be over. In a short ticae Mrs. Bruce and he were in animated conversation, and Lucy too was drawn into the chat. She looked for and noticed, tile opal ring on hie left, hand—the hand further from Mrs. Bruce and herielf. She quickly decided that,. although Rite had noticed his ring, he should not notice Itf.rs, and, under cover of the table, she twisted it toUnd so that the opal and curious part of the trittving came to the inside of her hand. As ha a.,lidressed the chief part of his ;remarks to lire_ Idt:tice, Lucy had time to observe him at by leisure. He was not a bad-looking man on Whole. He possessed fairly good features art* beautiful white teeth. His hair was almost black, and inclined to be curly, but his eyes attracted attention at once. It was not so much the colour, as the expression one noticed. They were dark and brilliant, and they seamed to have a strong ala.gnetic power. Lucy did not quite like to meet their full gale. He spoke with a slight American accent which did not detract from the interest of Itis speech. . _

"Boston, U. S. A.

"My dear Jack,—Enclosed in this letter you will find an opal ring, which I would have you always wear for my sake. It has a history. When I was twenty-five years of age I became engaged to a woman eliom I loved with all the strength of my nature. Even now that lam an old man wit grey hair, when I think of that time I am strangely moved. It is a long story. Our happiness was intero- We were to be married within a month's time, when we were cruelly parted by an enemy, who was jealous of our good fortune. We had had two rings made exactly alike, and we wore them during that short engagement. They were copied from an old one we admired in a collection. Well, we parted, and never met again until it wst too late. Years had passed away, we were both married, and had. afterwards discovered the fraud. At the bottom of the sheet Lucy found her own name and address.

"If I win I must marry her at once or the game is up. Delay means defeat."

The express from Liverpool to London could not fly fast enough for Jack Kemble. He threw himself back in the corner and tried to interest himself in the landscape, but his thoughts went back to the subject of his unrest.

THE "MEMBER FOR CYPRUS" SPEAKS.

Mr. R. Pierpoint's Concern for the Cypriotes.

In the House of Commons on Saturday, on the motion for the third reading of the Appropriation Bill, Sir Albert Rollitt asked for information regarding Cyprus, and suggested that that island could be used with advantage for cotton growing. Mr. Pierpoint regretted to hear Sir Albert Rollit make the statement that the island of Cyprus was a burden to this country. So far from the island being a burden, out of the revenues of Cyprus a large debt guaranteed by England and France was paid. Sir A. Rollit : I merely meant that owing to the payment of the tribute there is a balance against the Island which for the present is made up by the British exchequer. So far from being a burden the island is likely to be of great benefit to this country. Mr. Pierpoint said the Exchequer was relieved from the payment of .£41,000 and France from a similar amount under the guarantee of the loan of 1855. His hon. friend hoped that the tribute might be commuted, and if nothing better could be done he joined in the hope. He, however, called attention to the words in the original bond, "England swill pay." There was nothing whatever about "Cyprus will pay," and there was no consultation of the Cypriotes when the bond was entered into. As a legal question it could not be maintained that the tenants on the land were liable for the interest on it mortgage, but that was what the course adopted meant. Part of the original intention was that Cyprus should be made -a place d'arrnes and that there would be large Imperial etipendi- titre in the island, but that intention had been abandoned. A later idea which had been favourably considered by the late Colonial Secretary was that the island might usefully be made a free port and emporium for the Near East where goods might be received free of charge and whence they might be dis- tributed throughout Asia Minor. Merchants might azcummulate goods there, and deliver them in Turkish territory, and the island would be greatly benefited. Reports showed the difficulties the Cypriotes had to contend with from recurring drought, and it was rather hard that from an average revenue of £lBO,OOO they should be liable to pay a tribute of nearly £93,000. The consequence was that nearly every year Cyprus had to come for a grant in aid of £30,000. But this was after the payment of £41,000 to the 1855 bondholders, so that this country benefited to the amount of £11,600 Mr. Pierpoint waS about to draw attention to changes he considered desirable in the new rules,but the Speaker reminded hinithat this matter did not arise in discussion on the Bill.

The Colonial Secretary contended that great benefits had followed the remissions of taxation amounting to £190,000 in Cyprus. The Appropriation Bill was eventually read a third time amid cheers.

THE STRONG MAN OF THE EMPIRE.

An anonymous writer has a striking character study Of our new First Sea Lord, Sir John Arbuthnot Fisher, in the September number of the "Pall Mall Magazine." "To understand the nature of the task which Fisher," says the writer," has accomplished and that which he Is about to undertake, remember that the English fleet,un- like the Japanese, is hampered with obsolete survivals from a distant past. It is only a few months since the issue of boarding-pikes was discontinued to his Majesty's ships. Since the French, the Russians, and the Germans used iron or steel, boarding- pikes have been of no use, but their issue was continued for fifty years in our Navy after they were useless. This is the evil spirit which Sir John Fisher has had to cast out: because a thing existed in Nelson's time therefore it is right. The labours of Hercules were the pleasant solace of a summer day compared with the task of impregnating the Royal Navy with the scientific spirit. The work is not complete, but nine- tenths of what has been done is due to Sir John Fisher. The spirit of Nelson happily survives in full strength, but along with the spirit of Nelson there survive other things which are better dead. Fisher performed his gigantic task in silence. He made no speeches, with the exception of the diseourse at the Academy dinner ; he published no articles; he abhors pressmen; he does not advertise. At four o'clock every morning he is at his desk, and with the relentless persistence of machinery he coins will-power into work day after day, week after week, month after month, until at last he fills the greatest office in the Empire—that of First I Sea Lord—the man who orders the fleet."

HALF A SOVEREIGN IN HALVES.

A woman complained at Tottenham on Tuesday that when she offered half a sovereign—which was quite sound—in payment for a pair of shoes, the shopkeeper took it in his teeth and bitit in halves. The magistrate said he would be obliged to replace it.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible, not later than Thursday.

The Primrose League.

Sir,—"The Primrose League' is more in their way, with its silly sentimental title," so spake Mr. Joseph Chamberlain, once upon a time, and what he says, he -says he says and there's an end of it. Mi.. Joseph Chamberlain, speaking in the House of Commons, 29th, May, 1903, said something else: "If you are to give a preference to the Colonies . . . you must put a tax on food." Mr. Chamberlain must, however, be wronr, in that assertion because Mr. R. I. Wynne-Jones speaking at the Primrose League Fete, held at Walton Park, on Saturday last, is reported to have explained briefly what Mr. Chamberlain's planks in the political platform are and, somehow, his transla- tion hardly coincides with Mr. Chamberlain's. , More profit for the farmer, More employment for the labourer. And cheaper food for his family.' And then, Mr. Bottomley speaks and hurts the Liberals sorely. "He remembered saying in Warring- ton recently that their Liberal friends were lying like, gas meters, and he still held to that." Mr. Bottomley knows something and he ought to be good enough to enlighten the Liberals in their waywardness. He tells us what Mr. Chamberlain wants. "He had declared that his policy, if carried into effect, would not increase the price of food of either the richest or the poorest in the land" Wonderful! Itis quite clear evidently, to the members of the Primrose League that a tax on food will not increase the price. That is one of the disadvantages of ignoring figures—they, some- how, will tell the truth nnless they happen to be on gas meters, of course.—Yours Szc.,

BUTTERCUP. Rev. H. Dunnico and his Critics.

Sir,—Your correspondent W. H. Bell junior, has placed himself outside the realm of respectable discus- sion so I will content myself by stating that his state- ments are absolutely void of truth. Were I to imitate his methods of debate I should of course clothe this statement in language more forcible than polite but fortunately for my own self-respect I have a much less vulgar vocabulary at my disposal. There are three things I desire to mention. First Mr. Bell denies hav- ing written under half a dozen noms de plume. Well to be strictly accurate I desire to say that within a p.eriod of six or seven weeks he has used at least four different "signatures." Prior to that he had Ncritten anony- mously in more than one local paper. Your readers will therefore see that my statement which he denies is as near as it can be to truth. The second statement is that wherein he states that with my knowledge and consent be addressed a Bible class in connection with my own church. Mr. Pepper has already refuted that statement. I was not aware that he was expected until an hour or so before he was due and it was than too late to vetohis coming without placing him in a very awkward position. However I gave instructions that he must not be allowed to visit the class again and refused to attend and take part in the meeting. It is only fair to say that Mr. Pepper was not aware that Mr. Bell had visited Scotland-road Music Hall and found enjoyment there nor that he had publicly repudiated his belief in God. On the other hand it is inconceivable to me how any honest and consistent person could ask permission to take part in a meeting held for the sole purpose of worshipping" One" in whose existence he did not believe but this is quite in harmony with the ethics which govern his whole life. The third statement made is that wherein he would have your readers believe that he visited my house as a personal friend. I beg to say that Mr. Bell has simply availed himself of an invitation given to the hundreds of young men who attend my ministry. I may add here that Mr. Bell made such persistent efforts to absorb my time that on more than one occasion he has politely but firmly been refused admission. I treat his cheap sneers and false accusations with contempt, but at the same time pity the smallness of his mind. In conclusion may I point out that the one contention which I sought to prove—and which has drawn from Mr. Bell such a venomous and vitriolic attack—still awaits refutation, viz., that had your readers known that the "anonymous" correspondent who 'deemed himself com- petent to instruct and criticise "Christian Churches," "Christ's ' Example," "Political Clubs," "Social Brotherhoods," &c. ,2Pas W. H. Bell, a youth in his teens and one who found "Variety Stars " and "Music Hall ditties" more palatable to his refined and esthetic temperament than sermons which for months have attracted thousands of hard-headed honest working people then they. would have estimated the letter and its writer at their true worth.—Yours sincerely. HERBERT DUNNICO:

P.S.—T did not ascertain that "P. 8. was Miss 13eatrice Pemberton in confidence. it was public pro- perty long before I made use of the information.

[ln response to Mr. Dunnico's request we have sus- pended the closure of this correspondence in order that be might make the above personal explanation. The discussion must now,however, cease.—ED. " Ex."'

The Cost of "Conscription."

Sir,—The question of universal training for national defence is of such vital importance that anything which tends to confuse and mislead the public mind in the matter is to be deplored. Although the National Service League is not con- cerned to defend the recommendations of the Duke of Norfolk's Commission in favour of a year's training fot a home defence force, we feel compelled therefore to enter an emphatic protest against the extraordinary statement just issued, "showing the basis on which the estimated cost of £25,900,000 for Conscription was made." To begin with,the annual contingent is reckoned to be 380,000 men—the total number who reach the age of eighteen every year! Now, to realise the absurdity of such an estimate as the basis of a serious statement given in an official paper. we have only to set down the numbers nominally available in the case of the chief milit7ary powers (which have no regular army) and the numbers actually available for the Army and •Navy,after rejections on physical grounds, exemptions, emigra- tion, &.c., have been alloived for. The figures are (1) (2) (3) (4) Percent- age of Population. Nominally Actually column available. available. 3to 1. France ... 38.600,000 ... 324,000 ... 206,500 ... C• 53 Germany 58,000,000 ... 400,000 ... 360,000 ... o'sl Austria... Hungary 45,400,000 ... ? ... 125.000 ... 0'26 Italy 32,500,000 ... 348.000 ... 178,000 o'sl Russia -.143,000,000 ... 870,000 ... 651.000 --- (incl. Opolo- chenie) 219,000 (excl. Opolo- chenie

0•45

0•15

An annual contingent of 380,000 for the United King- dom would mean that nearly one per cent. of the whole population would be actually called up, i.e. nearly twice as many as in France, Germany and Russia, and four times as many as in Austria-Hungary! But the utter unreality of the basis given in this State document will only be fully appreciated when we remember that no allowance has been made for rejections on physical grounds and that, while the above contingents represent the recruits available for the whole military and naval , forces of the respective countries, not only our Regular Army but a Navy of 126,000 &lien has to be recruited from the 380,000 youths of eighteen. Now the ratio of rejections among those voluntarily presenting them- selves for enlistment is 38 per cent. (60 per cent. if rejections by recruiting officers are included). Taking the whole male population, the rejections would he at ' the least 40 per cent. leaving 228,000. Of these, 16,000 would have enlisted into the Navy, 40,000 would be needed for the Regular Army, about 18,000 would be serving in the Mercantile Marine, about 15,000 would be exempted on various gronnds, and at least 5.000 t would have emigrated (the number of emigrants of British birth is 206,000 annually). This would give 134,000 as the annual contingent of the home defence' forces, which with the numbers allowed for the recruitment of the Army and Navy would still be double the parcentage of men actually available for the Army and Navy in Austria-Hungary, and nearly as many as in France and Germany. We note that Mr. Arnold-Forster puts the cost of the conscript, home defence soldier at .£72 10s. per man, only 30s. less than that of the present regular soldier, and that he is to receive pay at Army rates, the patriotism of the English people being apparently considered far below that of Austrians, Swedes, Germans. Prussians,' Japanese, etc. Even so, this would give us £3,671,000 as the extra cost of a conscript Army, after deducting the cost of the Militia and Volunteers and 30,000 Regulars. But there can be no reason why we should regard the British people as the least patriotic in the world, and in that case the rate of £72 10s. Der head would be considerably reduced. and the £4.414,000 given by Mr. Arnold-Forster for doubling (!) the pay of the regular soldier would fall away, giving us a net gain of at least £1,000,000 on the annual estimates. it would be easy to show that this would be hut a small part of the advantage accruing frorp the adoption of national training for national defence. But we hone that the above examination of the official figures will undo some of the harm which must be caused by a fallaciousbasis on which the cost of universal training even in its most extreme form, has been presented to the people upon a matter of supreme importance, not only to the national safety, but to the physical, moral and industrial efficiency of the people. I have the honour to be, your obedient servant, GEORGE F. SHEE, Secretary, The National Service League. Mere House, Victoria-street, S.SV., 17th. August, 1904.

===== 0002601_19040820_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR MAGMIN'S HUGE PICNIC.

NEARLY 2,000 CHILDREN AT LYMM.

Councillor Maginn has established a record in War- rington as an organiser of children's picnics. On Thursday his second picnic for the school children of St. John's Ward was held and it was carried out on the same extensive scale as its predecessor of a year ago. Twenty-eight conveyances, comprising brakes and omnibuses and two steam traction engines drawing large waggons were requisioned to convey the large partly to Lymm, and it is quastionable whether such a sight as that i!oresented on Thursday has had its equal in the country. As 'bus after 'bus, ladea with smiling youngsters, proceeded on its way in what seemed an almost interminable procession, the onlooker could not fail to be impressed by the immensity of the undertaking and the responsibility attaching to this juvenile expedition. Councillor Maginn personally superintended the arrangements and they were as com- plete as his generosity to the children was bountiful. The conditions attaching to the picnic were that the children must reside in St. John's Ward and have attended school regularly for the first six months of this year. The number of children who qualified for the picnic was 1582 and this number together with some others who were allovv&I to join the picnic were safely conveyed to Lymm and back and entertained in a large field at Statham at Councillor Maginn's sole expense. Tea was also provided for 130 invited guests. The weather was beautifully fine, with the exception of a slight shower just as the children were preparing to leave for home. Swings and other attractions were provided free tot the children and it is a matter of course to say that they heartily enjoyed themselves. The duty of catering for this large company was under- taken by Mr. S. G. Collins, of the Spread Eagle Hotel, Lymm, and it was discharged satisfactorily. The chil- dren were provided with tea, bread and butter, buns, and current cake. Valuable assistance was rendered by some of the teachers from the schools, school attend- ance officers and friends, and it is satisfactory to record that not a single accident marred the day's enjoyment. A large number of parents journeyed by train to witness the proceedings. MR. MAGINN THANKED FOR HIS GENEROSITY. An excellent knife-and-fork tea was seryed to the invited guests in one of the large tents which had been erected. At the close Mr. John Dewhurst proposed a vote of thanks to Councillor Maginn. He explained that this year there was a proviso that those children who attended the picnic should make the full attendances at school, and the head teachers and assistants were astonished at the large number who had fulfilled the conditions. They were only sorry that those children who had made full attendances but who did not live in St. John's Ward were not able to attend the treat. He understood that Mr. Maginn's main object was to encourage- more regular attendance at school among the scholars, and his kindness had . increased the attendance to such an extent that the Government inspector had pointed out the overcrowded state of the schools. Mr. Maginn's kindness of heart was very great indeed. He had got them into a difficulty from overcrowding and perhaps it would not be too much to ask Mr. Maginn to build new schools in Warrington. (Laughter). Mr. Maginn: Out of the ratepayers' pocket. (Laughter). Mr. Dewhurst said apart from that, new schools wer6 needed and if they were provided he thought it would do away with a great deal of the sickness amongst chil- dren. (Hear, hear). He thanked Mr. Maeinn not only on behalf of the scholars but also on behalf of the friends who had attended. Mr. Edward Wallington seconded. It was his first vu,iton such an occasion, he said, and he must sap he was greatly surprised to see the very great benefit Mr. Maginn was conferring upon the town. In the first place he had brought these children out into the coun- try and they could hardly if realise how children who spent the whole of their lives in the town really en- joyed the outing. In the second place Mr. Maginn had given a very great help to the town beciuse the more tt-e children attended school the greater the Govern- ment grant and consequently the smaller the demand upon the rates. Every time a child attended school added the Government grant and the grant was completely lost if the children were absent. The education rate at the present time, he supposed, was between ninepence and tenpence in the £ but if every child in town attended school regularly he thought the education late would easily be reduced to sixpence in the pound, because the additional grant would exceed £2,000. In the third place Mr. Maginn was conferring a very great benefit upon the teachers because when the children attended school regularly the work of the teacher was materially reduced, as the children learnt more easily. For these reasons he would be very glad if there were more benevolent and philanthropic men in Warrington like Mr. Maginn so that the picnic could be extended to all the childi en in the town. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Upson said he could bear testimony to the kind heartedness of Councillor Maginn in providing that treat for the children. After emphasising the remark of Mr. Wallington that other gentlemen would follow Mr. Maginn's example, Mr. Upson expressed the opinion that the picnic was not altogether the outcome of Mr. Maginn's desire to reduce the rates but it had come out of his kindness of heart. (Hear, hear.) The vote was carried with acclamation. Mr. Maginn in acknowledging the compliment, said it was stated twelve months ago that he had an ulterior object in giving the picnic and that was to assist his candidature for St. John's Ward. That was not his object, and could not be his object now, for he was a member of the Council. He regretted that his offer of a year ago that he would be one of ten gentlemen to give a treat to every little boy and girl who attended school regularly had Rot been taken up. It might be thought that the expense entailed would be entirely lost, but he had proved that the money was more than repaid by the additional grants which were earned as the result of the improved attendance. By attending school nine times more a year than they would by irregular attendance,each child would earn a shilling additional grant and that shilling would pay for the cost of the picnic. It also had the advantage of assisting the school attendance officers in their work. He held that a child who attended school by force was not as good a scholar as the one who went voluntarily. By encouraging regular school attendance they were also reducing the burden of the ratepayer. He had now given the picnic two years in succession and if be did not repeat it another year it would not be through any lack of desire on his part but through lack of funds. (Hear, hear). Mr. MAGINN acknowledged the assistance of the caterer, a compliment which Mr. Collins acknowledged.

THE SMOKE NUISANCE.

Messrs. Crosfield and Sons and the Mayor.

[TO THE EDITOR OF THE " EXAMINER:I Sir.—We shall be obliged if you will insert in your next issue the letters enclosed herewith. The corres- pondence speaks for itself, and requires no further com- ment from us.—Thanking you in anticipation, yours faithfully, JOSEPH CROPSY.FIE LD & SONS, LTD. CO

15th August, 1904.

His Worship the Mayor, Town Hall, Warrington. Dear Sir,—We notice, as reported in the " Warring- ton Guardian " of Saturday last, that in your speech at the Town Council meeting last Tuesday evening, you made a statement to the effect that we are sending out sulphuric acid instead of black smoke from our works chimneys. As this statement has no foundation in fact, and as we are sure you would not intentionally do us any injury, we shall be glad if ycu will retract the charge as publicly as you have made it. The writer of Town and Country Notes" in the same paper, under the same date, without taking the slightest trouble to verify the statement, has assumed it to be correct, and has made what we consider to be an extremely objectionable comment thereon. A criticism of this character, following upon your remarks, is calculated to damage our reputation, and we have no doubt you will do whatever is necessary to put the matter right forthwith. If you can furnish us with any information as to the source from which you were supplied with the particu- lars which caused you to make the inaccurate statement referred to, we should be very much obliged for the same. We will take the liberty of sending this letter to the Local Press, together with your reply.—We beg to remain yours faithfully, JOSEPH CROSFIELD & SONS, Ltd.

COPY. Offi Town Clerk's ce, Warrington. 16th Aupst, 1904.

Dear Sirs,-1 am directed by the Mayor to acknow- ledge the receipt of your letter of the 15th instant and to inform you that the statement made by him in the Council that works chimneys were emitting sulphuric acid was not intended to have special reference to your works, as he had not your firm in his mind although the previous speaker had been referring to your works, and he regrets that a contrary intention has been inferred from the report of the proceedings of the Council. His Worship has no objection to your making use of this reply in any way you think proper.—Yours faithfully, J. LYON WHITTLE.' Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons Ltd., Bank Quay, Warrington.

The Examiner, Saturday, August 20, 1904

or drunkenness, or improvidence, be quartered on the rates? I hear some queer stories about those who are luck- y enough to be kept by the Corporation. If a horse 'lnds itself there any day, it knocks off half its work, and slows down to half speed, and takes life by the smooth handle afterwards.

Those of us who work bard, and pay rates and taxes, and keep the wolf from the door by ceaseless effort, cannot afford to set up an asylum at our own expense for the lazy, and the incompetent, and drunken loafers who do not believe in work. These dregs of society should be sent to some labour colony, five miles from any public-house, and they should live on what they earn, and no more. I would sternly repress our" Tired Tims," and " Weary Willies," and the vast army of tramps and loafers who live like parasites on the workers. And I would reserve our municipal and parochial charities for the maimed, and the halt, and the blind, and the fatherless, and the aged poor. If any man thinks I am hard on a certain section of our unemployed, who tramp from Land's End to John O'Groats in search of a job and never manage to find one, he must remember that I know them, and I am up to some of their little games. And familiarity breeds, not pity, hut contempt.

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.I

SOCIAL SERMONETTES FOR WORKING MEN.

BY JOHN TAYLOR.

Author of "Search Lights on Land, Labour

and Social Problems."

===== 0002601_19040820_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CRUSADE AGAINST WORK.

"What men want is not talent, but purpose; not the power to achieve, but the will to labour."— LYTTON.

We have among us to-day some foolish and mischiev- ous teachers who are doing infinite mischief to the cause of labour. They are teaching men that work is a degrading thing, and that all the workas who are com- pelled to earn their own living by the sweat of their brow are little better than slaves. They are telling men that capitalists exploit their labour, and rob them of a large proportion of their earnings, and that the worker should take life easier' and put in less time, and when he loses his situation he should quarter himself on the municipality or the State. and insist on being main- tained at the public expense. Now if these statements were true I should be one of the first and foremost to, seek some relief for our oppressed workers, and to better their condition in life. But I believe they are absolutely false, and lam per- suaded that the lot of the average worker is largely what he makes it. And I believe if we quartered our unemployed on the State or on the municipality, the remedy would be ten times worse than the disease.

_ I ex-pect I shall get into trouble for uttering these sentiments, but I have a duty laid on my conscience, and I must protest against any gospel of laziness and inefficiency and vindicate the cairns of honest labour. If I stir up a few wasps' nests in the operation,so much the better. If I set men thinking Phave done my duty, and I shall sleep the sleep of the just. I.—WORK IS NOT A DEGRADING THING.

All honest, intelligent work is ennobling and inspir- ing. Let a man be keenly interested in his daily duties, and give him the will to labour, and he will feel that life is worth living. He will have something to think about and look forward to that will inspire him with hope and keep his head above water. There is always hope for the man who is willing to work. It is the man who flings down his tools and says it is no use try- ing again that usually dies in despair. Thomas Carlyle was one of the hardest workers of his time, and his profoundest truths were woven from his bitter experience of /ife. He spent months and years in writing his "History of the b'rench Revolution," and when he had finished it, John Stuart Mill borrowed the manuscript and sat up one night reading it. When he went to bed he left the precious papers on the table, and a careless servant lit the fire with them next morning. What that loss meant to a poor author nobody can tell. Carlyle was strick dumb by the awful shock, and for a fortnight he hap:lly spoke a word. Mill gave him one hundred pbunds to keep the wolf from the door, and when he saw that his immediate wants were likely to be met, he sat down and wrote a new history that he said was better than the one that had been destroyed. A man who has had such rough experience of life as this,may well regard honest work as the worker's salva- tion. He says: "All work, even cotton spinning, is noble. Work is the mission of man on this earth. A day will arrive when he who has no work to do, by whatever name he may be named, will not find it good to show himself in our quarter of the solar system, but may go and look out elsewhere if there be an idle planet discoverable. Let the honest working man rejoice that such law, the first of nature, has been made good to him, and hope that by-and-bye all else will be made good. It is the beginning of all. Let wastefulness, idleness, drunkenness, improvidence take the fate which God has appointed them." If you doubt the wisdom and truth of Carlyle's teaching you had better look round you and see what samples of manhood and womanhood are produced by the teachings of our pothouse politicians and our gutter reformers. Go through the streets of any of our large towns any night after factory hours and see how our young people are wasting their precious time and de- moralising themselves by bad company and degrading habits. Our future hnsbands and wives are graduating in the music-hall and the public-house, and they are learning manners among Hooligans in the streets. If I had my way they would be at work, learning to mend their own clothes, and cook a decent meal, and keep a house clean and tidy. And yet we have self-appointed prophets among us who are everlastingly advocating less work, and shorter hours, and a lower standard, of efficiency, and more recreation and frivolity. The men who are carrying on the crusade against work are as dangerous to the community as the gambling hell or the ginshop. Our young people can get into lazy, drunken, extravagant habits without any teaching, and the men who lead them astray should be sternly dealt with.

11.-INTELLIGENT WORK IS USUALLY THE

FORERUNNER OF SUCCESS.

It is the ignorant, unskilled, untrained worker that comes to grief. There must be some proportion between the means used and the end that is aimed at, or failure will be assured. A man stopped me the other day and asked me to buy some worthless notepaper and some useless pens. He had a small parcel of these articles wrapped up in brown paper, and he was tramp- ing from door to door in the hope of making a living. If he had stolen all his stock-in-trade and sold it ata fair market price he could not have realised enough to pay him for his trouble. His trade was fore-doomed to failure, and he knew it, but it gave him the excuse for a bit of cadging, and that is important to the men who are always on the road" and everlastingly "stoney broke."

The men who founded most of our factories and work- shops, and industrial concerns rose from the ranks of the workers. They were Door enough when they started, and they worked a great deal harder than any of their workpeople. They did not lie in bed till eight, and go to business at nine or ten every morning, and leave off at four in the afternoon. They did not spend their evenings at the theatre, or in the public-house,or in the boozing club, and their week-ends at the seaside, and their holidays on the Continent. They had to watch their pennies and eat the bread of carefulness, and they rose from the ranks by sheer hard work, and steadfast purpose, and unflinching self-denial, and these moral qualities were the making of them. Dr. Aitkin says: "An eminent manufacturer of those days used to be in his office before six o'clock every morning, accompanied by his children and his apprentices. At seven o'clock they all cam" to break- fast, which consisted of one large dish of water por- ridge, made of oatmeal and a little salt,boiled thick,and poured into a dish. At the side was a basin of milk,and the master and his apprentices, each with a wooden spoon in his band, without loss of time dipped into the same dish, and then the milk, and aa soon as they had finished they all returned to their work." The men who laid the foundations of our great industrial enterprises knew before they started that they would have to make great sacrifices, and they would have to work hard and deny themselves if they were to succeed. So they took a lively intelligent interest in their work, and they found to their surprise, that they were not only building up factories and workshops that would enrich them and employ their neighbours, but they were building up characters for themselves. It is this element of personal character that our pot- house politicians and our gutter prophets overlook. You can find a donkey that can carry a bigger load than a man, but you can never make that donkey into a man. You can get a machine that will do certain kinds of work better than any man, but you can never invest that machine with human qualities and give it intelli- gence. So long as men and women are content to com- pete with their lowest rivals they must not complain about competition. If a man has a good trade in his fingers, and has a practical. Intelligent head on his shoulders, and is willing to work, he ought to know his way about sufficiently to be his own employer, and strike out a new and original line of life for himself. If such a man ever becomes a slave to anybody it will be his own fault. fie has the mental, and physical, and moral qualities that lead to success in life, and there are thousands of our best w,drkers who are travelling that road to-day. lII.—COMPETITION ALWAYS HITS THOSE HARDEST WHO CAN DO THE LEAST.

Perhaps you will be offended with me if I look at this question through economic spectacles fora moment. If a man is only fit to do a donkey's work the economist will say that he should be content to feed on grass and thistles and have a bed of straw. If he is competing with a steam- engine he should be able to live on coal and water. A man's value to-day does not depend so much on his size, and weight, and strength as it de- pends upon his intelligence, and ability, and efficiency. I often wonder whether the young people of this generation are making donkeys and machines of them- selves, or they are trying to make themselves into real men and women. Take a score of the ignorant young loafers who rush off every night as soon as work is done, and spend the evening in the streets or in the public. house, and cAompel them to pass some sort of examina- tion, and you will be surprised how little they know. Beyond their daily round and common task in the factory, or the forge, they are RS ignorant as boat-horses If work is slack, and they lose their situations, theyare done for. They cannot turn their hands to any other trade, so they cut each other's throats and pull down wages in their own particular branch of industry. The remedy for this keen competition is a wise and generous extension of our educational system, with pewer to compel all ;young people under sixteen years of age to attend evening continuation classes and pass satisfactory examinations. Knowledge is power, and any worker with brains and industry may soon get on in the ladder of progress, and rise beyond the reach of the keenest competition at the bottom. Why should any able-bodied worker who has wasted his opportunities, and demoralised himself by idleness,

===== 0002601_19040820_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHILDREN'S SUMMER CAMP.

Interesting Report by Mr. W. H. J.

Jenkins,

The Hon. Secretary to the Warrington Children's Summer Camp,Mr. W. H. J.Jenkins,has kindly favoured us with an advance copy he has prepared of the annual report to be presented at a meeting of subscribers. The report reads as follows : n an article which appeared a few days since in a leading journal on the report of an inter-departmental committee on the physical decay and loss of stamina amongs certain classes of the British people, it was observed while there seems to be reason for accepting the conclusion of the committee that there is not suffici- ent ground for the belief that there is any general pro- gressive physical deterioration, there is revealed never- theless a serious falling away in the fitness of certain classes, and a great number of the people who are wholly dependent for subsistence on their own exertions are bodily unfit and incapable of making those exertions properly. Not merely are many of the poorer classes unfit q to fight in their country's battles, but they are positively unfit to bear their own part in the battle of life."

Conclusions such as these give rise to serious concern as apart from purely moral considerations, the physical well-being of any and every class in the community is a matter of supreme interest to every citizen. It will I trust Rot be thought inappropriate that the obser Mons before quoted form other than a fitting preface to my report on the work carried on by the Committee of the "Children's Summer Camp" during the present season. Having secured (after no inconsiderable amount of trouble) the tenancy of four acres of land, the property of Lady McLaren, abutting on the sea shore and with a splendid prospect of the Welsh hills to the southward at Towyn, in the parish of Meliden, near Prestatyn, the work of organization was begun. Arrangements were at once made for a supply of drinking water being laid on to the camp, the erection of the necessary cook's shanty and storeroom with and out offices necessary for the officers and children. Having regard to the uncertainty of the finances, it was decided to make use of two marquees given by a friend for officers' quarters and mess room, bell tents being requisitioned for the children. Sonie of these were also used subsequently by the officers being found secure, comfortable and wind proof. The services of the same staff as last year were secured.—Mr. F. J. Emson, officer in charge, Messrs. F. H. Aukland, Alexander, Malcolm, Gordon Dawson, Inspeotor Hackett, Alexander Chappell (in charge of tents) and James Macfarlane (cook). During the third week the services of Messrs. Ryan and Seed were obtained in lieu of two officers who were unable to stay longer. It suffices to say that the efforts of all the officials, act- ing as officers, whose services were gratuitous' deserve the fullest appreciation, and all who worked to make the camp life both for the children and those engaged in what were certainly arduous duties go smoothly and enjoyably, merit the warmest thanks and recognition. When it is borne in mind that the cooking and serving of over 300 meals a day, in addition to other multifar- ious duties had to be performed, the working of a camp is not quite " a playground for the officials "as some uncharitable persons have been kind enough to indicate. The first batch of boys were sent tt camp on the 15th July. Headed by the band of the Blue Coat SchoolBo children assembled at the Drill Shed by the kind permission of Lieutenant Colonel Edelsten and pro- ceeded to Bank Quay Station and thence to Prestatyn. The same methods were adopted each week. Thus 84 boys were despatclied on the 22nd July and 96 on the 29th July the last contingent of boys thus ending their holiday on the sth August. There is not room within the scope of this report to give more than a brief analy- sis of the pleasures afforded by camp life. Bathing in the sea! country side and seaside jaunts amid rural scenery and healthful surroundings ; instructive lessons given by trained men in natural history, physical geography and the geological formations in the district which are perhaps from the abounding mineral strata as interesting as any part of the British islands. The ex- periment of taking a batch of girls to camp having been decided upon the work was Approached with some degree of misgiving as to its likelihood of success. A volunteer lady superintendent was found in the person of Mrs. Wilkinson. with the following eight volunteer lady officers :—Misses Emson, Bailey, Haley, Laurie, Dickenson. Fairclough,Jefferson, and Taylor. To these , officers our very special thanks are due, as without any previous experience Mrs. Wilkinson undettook a very responsible post, and most ably fulfilled the duties, the young lady officers cheerfully and with ungrudging devotion giving her every possible support and assist- ' ance. Seventy-two girls assembled at the Drill Shed on the sth August, and with their ' officers marched in orderly fashion with many mothers and friends to the Irailway station at Bank Quay, and from thence landed safely in camp. There they braved" the tempest and the breeze "like heroes. I have everyreason to believe that both the children and the officers spent a most enjoyable week, the storm of the early part of the lweek being forgotten in the health-giving experience of the remaining part of the holiday. • I venture to think that the girls have won a per- manent place in our holiday programme. The Hon. Treasurer (Mr. A. H. Lee) and some members of his family during the week the girls were in camp took a practical interest in the work an.l rendered all the assistance in their power to make matters go smoothly by living in camp and taking a full share in the neces- sary duties and detail of camp life. The girls' week being of an experimental character they were exceed- ingly anxious it should pass off without hitch or mis- hap. " All's well that ends well," and the girl's week is not the least satisfactory of the experiences of the season's work.

It is sincerely hoped that the efforts made by the committee will be attended with a large measure of success in assisting to build up the physical condition of the children and increasing their stamina and fitting them to fight the battle of life successfully and become good and useful citizens. That has been the primary aim of the work. There is every reason to believe that it has been attended with very satisfactory results. Both the hon. treasurer and myself have taken every possible interest in the work. The Director of Educa- tion (Mr. J. Moore Murrey) and his staff have also rendered willing and important service for which our greatest thanks are due. I cannot close this report without thanking Mr. Linnell (Lady McLaren's agent), Mr. and Mrs. Ellis and many others at Prestatyn for their uniform kindness and assistance during our stay there. I was in attendance each iVeek end and saw that all necessary provision for the comfort and well-being of those in camp was made. The genial weather has made the camp work much easier to carry out than under less auspicious circumstances. I would however impress upon you the necessity of making provision in the way of shelter for the children to play and take their meals, and also for holding evening meetings should there be a spell of inclement weather.

HATTON HORTICULTURAL SHOW. NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. List of Prizes.

The tenth annual show promoted by the Hatton Amateur Horticultural Society was held on Thursday at Hatton Hall, the residence of Mr. James Parr. The weather was beautifully flue, in striking contrast to the wet weather of a year ago, which militated against the success of the show. Forty pounds had been offered in prizes and there was keen competi- tion in some of the classes. The exhibits showed a great improvement, both in quality and quantity. The exhibits were shown in tw6 tents. There was a fine display of cut flowers, and a special feature was the artistically arranged dinner tables, for which special prizes had been offered. The Walton Brass band was in attendance, and played a selection of music. Tea was served in a tent in charge of the Misses Dar- linaton.

In addition to the indoor exhibits, prizes were offered for the best outdoor' exhibits. Mr. John Kippax was an easy first for the best stocked and neatest flower garden. The opening ceremony was performed by Mrs. Lyon of Appleton Hall, and amongst those present were Lady Greenall, Mrs. Wynne-Jones, Mr. and Mrs. John White, Mr. J. E. Seeker, Rev. F. Clifton Smith, Hon. and Rev. C. F. Cross, Mrs. and Miss Pigot, Miss Milner. Miss Groome, Mrs. and Misses Blair, Mrs. Fletcher, Dr. Bower. Mr. T. Baxter, C.C., Mr. and Mrs. Rumney, Mrs. and Miss Ridgway,Mrs . Bowden, Miss Braids and others. In declaring the shoe open, Mrs. Lyon spoke upon the uses to which flowers could be put. Not only were they used at church festivals and in beautifying the homes, but they performed a useful service in the hospitals by making the wards more cheerful. The beautiful crosses and wreaths at funerals also touched their hearts and reminded them of the rssurrection. In speaking of the homely vegetable Mrs. Lyon expressed the opinion that in England it was under-valued. In other countries, vegetables were more generally used and there was no doubt that they were excessively nutritious. She did not think that an excessive meat diet was very good and a greater use of vegetables was desirable. (Hear. hear). The Rev. F'. Clifton-Smith proposed a vote of thank to Mrs. Lyon, and in referring to the work of the Horticultural Society, he said anyone going through Hatton could not fail to see a great improvement in the gardens. Mr. R. Warburton seconded the vote of thanks and it was unanimously carried. Mrs. Lyon acknowledged the compliment. Lady Greenall intimated to the Secretary (Hr. Hatton) that next year she would offer a special prize for button- holes and table decorations for cottagers. the flowers to be grown in their own gardens. THE PRIZE LIST. THE JUDGES.

A retired Fletchertown collier, Eilbeck Brown, over 70 years of age, was run into and killed by a train on the Bolton Loop Line, near Mealsgate Station, Cumber- land, on Wednesday morning. Mr. Brown was for 44 years a local preacher of the Wigton Primitive Metho- dist Circuit. At one time he was an ardent cocktighter and it is related that on being converted he killed the cock,and invited the minister to help him to eat it.

Cricket.

The Warrington team has either struck a bad vein or islfinding its level very rapidly.lf the truth be candidly admitted I think the all-round strength of the side was rather over-estimated in the earlier part of the season. They were not quite so powerful as their all-conquering record tended to show, but played with a confidence which was worth both runs and wickets every time they stepped on the field. Once the spell was broken and Warrington descended to the position of a good Saturday eleven, while other clubs took them boldly in band. Bootle, and now Ashton-under-Lyne, have both hand- somely avenged severe drubbings inflicted by Warring- ton during the team's hey-day of success. On Saturday the batting of the Warrington team was of the tamest description, and only one player reached double figures, this being Bibby who compiled 25—just half of War- rington's paltry score.

1 This is anticipating events somewhat. The visitors I were the first to go to the wickets, and made a very ; unpromising start. The Ashton captain played the first ball sent down in a manner which seemed to indicate that he was not ready to begin. At any rate Lynes got I his %wicket and continued to bowl with such judgment land accuracy as to secure fctur wickets by the time 25 I runs had been put together. Norman S. Eaton was the I new comer and he hit out vigorously. He, however, lost ' his partner through a smart return from E. E. Woods rat extra cover,which caused the retirement of Threlfall who was run out whilst venturing on a second run. On H. Coop and Eaton becoming associated, a long stand Iwas made. Eaton gave a chance to Connor which was not taken and from that point to the end of the innings ' Eaton collared the bowling completely and hit out freely. He was especially severe on the bowling of Lynes, and spoiled the professional's average, his four wickets costing 57 runs. When the Ashton innings was declaredclosed, they had run up a score of 174 for seven wickets, N. S. Eaton being chief contributor with a not out innings of 86.

Warrington had less than two hours in which to save the match, but their display at the wickets was so indifferent that in a little over an hour, the whole team was dismissed for 50 runs. Podmore was given run out in the first over—a palpable mistake on the part of the umpire. When eight had been scored Lynes played on to his wicket. J. %V. Bibby came in and showed greater confidence in his treatment of the bowling than any of his associates. He got little assistance from any of the other batsmen, and at the close of the innings he was 25 not out. Hewetson and Bailey bowled unchanged throughout the innings and had every reason to be satisfied with their performance. The "trimmers" sent down by Hewetson were especially dangerous and he secured the best wickets. He obtained five wickets for 28 runs whilst Bailey got four for 17—two of these wickets being obtained in the last over. The turf was in very good condition, a fact which redounds to the credit of groundsman Denton.

The second team made the journey to Ashton with only eight of the regular players. The umpire, R. Hayes, was called upon to fill one of the vacancies, and two other substitutes were found on the field. Never- theless this depleted team made a more creditable show thth the first team, the match ending in a draw. J. Thompson and W. Hind by a strange coincidence each contributed 45, and double ?'figures were also reached by F. Pierpoint, A. Duckworth, and C. D. Bromley. The innings closed for 166. Ashton responded with 118 for five wickets, Thompson proving himself as cap9.ble with the ball as he had been with the bat. His bowling aver- age was 4 wickets for 52 runs.

The Grappenhall eleven are to be congratulated upon their clever performance at Northwich. To visit the salt centre and return victorious must be booked down as one of the most creditable wins of the season, and avenges the defeat at Grappenhall. Mr. A. L. Kaye, came out top scorer with 22 and largely contributed to the victory, which was only by the narrow margin of 6 runs. It sufficed nevertheless.

The visit of Sankey Wire Mills to Haydock Lodge was disastrous to the wire-men. It may be that the company in which they found themselves was not very agreeable and that their nerves were upset. At any rate the Lodge men gained the verdict by 13 runs. Dr. Cobban was their chief contributor with 25 runs. 11. Cook and Woodcock were the only men on the visit- ing side to reach double figures.

Matters were pretty even at Moore Park, where Moore and Daresbury entertained Walker's brewery team. The home eleven were all dismissed for 80 runs, and 37 of the 80 were from the bat of C. B. Allen. When stumps were drawn the brewery team were 49 for 5 wickets, W. Ashton carrying out his bat for a useful 22 runs.

Stretton and Appleton cut but a poor figure against St. Paul's. The villagers were all dismissed for 31 runs and then St. Paul's, or at least, S. Mason gave them some leather hunting, the churchmen having run up 95 runs for the loss of 9 wickets when the closure was applied. Of this total Mason was tesponeible for 52 runs.

Mersey-street proved themselves too good for the Golborne-street Baptists. Mersey-street is perhaps more notable for its tanyards than its cricketers, but on this occasion the cricketers carried off the honours. After running up a score of 95, they brought out the Baptists for 45, Rutter and Hunter dividing the bowl- ing honours between them.

The Brewery second team was badly beaten by the second team of Sankey Wire Mills. The winning team was in such good form with the bat that 153 runs were made for the loss of four wickets only. B. Taylor (55) and George Gough (38 not out) were the chief con- tributors. The brewery men were all dismissed for 38 runs, Bladen capturing 7 wickets for 11 runs.

Crosfleld's. I hear, got the better lf the Penketh team, but as no score has been sent in I am unable to give details.

Bowling.

The Warrington and district league competition has been practically settled, although there are still a few matches to be played. A glance at the league table will suffice to show that the Cemetery club are certain of victory. They have a clear four points lead, and as no other club in the league can score 20 points this season, the members of the Cemetery Club are entitled to claim that they have already won the cup and medals. They are to be congratulated upon their performance, and very few will be prepared to say that they have not thoroughly deserved the honour. They practically made their position secure on Saturday when they defeated the Horse and Jockey Club by a majority of 21 points. The closest games were those between Swanton and G. Holbrook of the Cemetery team and T. Wells and G. Higham of the Horse and Jockey side. In both con- tests the Horse and Jockey men secured the victory by a margin of two points. The match between J.Harrison (Cemetery) and T. Harrison (Horse and Jockey) was a run away game for the latter, he securing the victory by a margin of 8 points. T. Berry (Cemetery) however, seemed the largest margin by defeating A. Shelmerdine by 12 points. B. Taylor (Cemetery) also obtained an 8 pointsvictory over his opponent, D. Daniels.

Two close neighbours—Sankey and Penketh---Igave good account of themselves. Sankey obtained the points by a margin of 25. There was a keenly-fought game between J. Plumpton (Sankey) and H. Beyer (Penketh) the last named triumphing by a single point. H. Houghton and A. Mynekyme largely contributed to the victory of the Sankey men by two hollow victories, which in themselves loft a margin for the Chapel House men of 22 points.

Fearnhead are dangerous competitors when operating on their own turf, as the Brooklands team learnt to their cost. The Fearnhead trundlers succeeded in winning eleven out of the twelve games and piled up a majority of 94 points. Penketh Park are the visitors to Feamhead to-day and if they share the same fate as Brooklands they will be in close proximity to the wooden spoonisbs.

The Liberal Club team seems destined to be the pos- sessors of the wooden spoon for this season at all events. They stand at the bottom of the league with eight points. The two remaining matches are away from their own green, and the llull's Head and Fearnhead teams will no doubt gain points at their expense.

The following is the league table up-to-date : P. W. L. For Agst. Pts. Cemetery Bull's Head Chapel House Horse and Jockey l3 ... 6 ... 7 ...1933 ...1763 ... 12 Fearnhead Brooklands Penketh Liberal Club l2 ... 4 ... 8 ...1703 ...1835 ... 8

To-day's league matches are : Horse and Jockey v. Brooklands. Fearnhead V. Penketh Park. Bull's Head v. Liberal Club.

A meeting of the Warrington and District League was held at the Liberal Club on Wednesday evening, Mr. G. F. Freeman presiding. The opening of the 'Victoria Park bowling green was under discussion. It has been arranged to open the green on the 3rd of September,and as announced in this column last week, the League will play a match with a team from the Works Association. The teams are to be sixteen eagle, and the Mayor and Deputy Mayor will be asked to captain the respective teams.

The annual bowling handicap in connection with the league will take place on the ground of the Warrington Cricket and Bowling Club on September 10th when over 200 entries are anticipated and valuable prize will be competed for.

The magnificent cup and medals which have been won

by the Cemetery club in the league competition are OM view in the window of Messrs. Eustance and CompanY-

There was a pleasant gathering in connection with the Cemetery Bowling Club on Thursday evening, when the prizes given annually by Mr. A. H. Crodleld were dis- tributed. Mr. Kaye (chairman of the committee) pre- sided over the first part of the proceedings and Mr. S. Taylor took the chair during the latter portion of the. evening. Several toasts were proposed, including that of the visitors which was proposed by Mr. J. Willett. The donor of the prizes was heartily thanked for his generosity. During the evening songs were contributed by Messrs. Draper, Humphries, Davies, T. Healing and others.

The second round in the Works Bowling Competition was completed on Saturday, and there was some clos competition for entry into the third round. The handi- cap will not be proceeded with to-day, but on the 27th inst. it is hoped to complete the handicap and award the prizes.

Cycling. The members of the Warrington Cycling Club had a run to Oulton Park on Saturday. The weather was dull and threatening for this most popular fixture with the result thatl rather less than a score of members turned out. The ride across Daimler° Forest was greatly en- joyed, the gorse and heather just now being at its very bet. Tea was served at the Red Lion, after which ese• members had a pleasant stroll through the park and the village. The return journey was rather marred by several showers but Warrington was eventually reached after a most enjoyable afternoon's outing.

Tuesday's run was to Cronton. The weather was - rather gloomy but it continued fine. Only eleven mem- bers joined in the run. They came back through Widnes so that they might take advantage of a rail-vayjournff' in the event of rain. Fortunately the services of the rail- waycompany were not required.

To-day's run is to that favourite spot—Cranage, where- tea will be partaken of at the Swan Farm. On Tuesday' Warburton will be visited.

LIGHTING-UP TIME.

To-night 8.29 Sunday 827 Monday 8.25 Tuesday 8.23 Wednesday Thursday Friday.

The Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Cycling Club's run ou Saturday was to have been to Delamere, but owing to the Band of Hone trip to Frodsham the run was altered to that place. The weather was all that could be desired and twentyone members turned out. The return journey home was made at 7.30. After having a splendid time at the top of the hill, Warrington was reached at 10 o'clock, after two small mishaps which were not very serious.
