Angling.

Anglers will he pleased if the rain continneq. for rivers and streams are greatly in need of water. .ty nothing of the scarcity for business purposes. There are many complaints about low and clear streams and unfavour- able weather. In a few days we shall no doubt hear of salmon and sea trout ascending in great numbers. For weeks they have been waiting for heavy waters.

Each week end sees the approaching end of the big' events of the season. Last week the Warrington Angling' Association concluded their annual contest at Moore. On the 27th at Thelwall they will dispose of the annual 41 medal competition.

Water Polo.

- --- - The Warrington first team is makinga bold bid for the cl,allenge bowl of the Liverpool and District I.4eagile Association (second division). They have already won two matches away and beaten Everton here, and they stand at the top of the league for the present, On Tuesday they journey to Southport for a friendly match.

Wootton and Warrington second team met in the third division comnetition for the Vol Smith Shield in Wootton Bath on Tuesday evening. The game developed into a complete farce,apparently owing to the inability' of the visitors, who are familiar with a large bath, to• adapt themselves to the small oval-shaved bath at Wootton. Six goals were scored by Wootton, three 10 each half, the scorers being J. Martin (four), Foster and Lott. In the course of the game. Warren, one of the Warrington players had no leave the water owing to- a slight injury the teams being equalised in strength b..? a Wootton player (Rylands) being withdrawn.

The defeat of the Warrington second team practically destroys any hope they may have had of retaining the shield which they won laat year for the first time. Thor are now at the bottom of the league. Oa Tuesday theY play Birkenhead a league match in the Baths, Le& street.

===== 0002601_19040820_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
Football.

A fortnight to-day and the clash of battle will be" heard resounding from the football field. How soon the close season has passed! It needs but a very short. step into the past to take us in imagination to the scene of the Northern Union Cup final which rung down the curtain on the 1903-4 tournament, and—berg we ar.o again! The Warrington men have been getting their muscles loosened already. Two practices have been held, with encouraging turn-outs, and there will be another this evening. Many of the players have signed on, and what is even more interesting, the committee have obtained the signatures of a couple of new forwards from whom great things are expected.

It is gratifying to find that the Club managers have taken time by the forelock and endeavoured to strengthen the team in its weakest places during the period of no-play. For an important capture to be made late on in the season causes almost as much annoyance as satisfaction. The two new hands' are Shugars, who has already been alluded to in this column, 'and Joe Belton, the well-known Birkenhead forward. Both are big, powerful men and should do much tow. irds solidifying the shaky IVarrington pack. Shugars is about 14st. 10lbs. in weight, while Belton comes in astone lighter,and 6ft. 2in. in height. These are men of the right stamp, and together with Boardman and Thomas they should make an excellent vanguard.

During the coming season, the captaincy of the first team will be in the reliable hands of staid Jack Hallarnt and I don't think amore popular choice could have been made. Tsherwood has had a good run as skipper. and doubtless will be only too pleased to hand over the wheel to another. G. Dickenson is also among the honours, the post of sub-captain having been allotted to him. The A "team will be led by E. Brooks, the centre-threequarter who has several times assisted the premier team.

The retirement of Tom Broadley is again announced, and this time there seems reason to believe that he has' given up the game altogether. Still I should not be surprised, if the Bradford club struck a bad patch during the coming season, to see the burly international once; more showing the scrummagers how to pack, push au' rush. There have not been many better forwards thau Broadley, and his worth to the Bradford teams of the past isAincalculable. Opponents have always had goOd reason to fear Tom's 15 stone rush, for it generally' carried him over the line and spelt points. He Or, bath a good player and a good captain, and he has we earned a rest.

versa, is but a temporary expedient, and does not strike at the root of the matter. 'To furnish electrical energy to these electric lines (for London) some thousands of horse power in generating stations would be needed. Now the chief reason for the daily exodus of the people is the smokiness and confine- ment of city quarters, but this condition is chiefly caused by steam-driven factories and domestic chimneys. The large amounts of power needed to drive the railway trains migilt with advantage be turned to operate the steam-ariven works by elec- tric power when the first evil would be rnittgated. But this only partially solves the problem, and we look to the work of the great electric power com- panies toinaugurate a new industrial era which shall bring about the decettraliz4tion of she work- ing classes and confine the trade to sections of workers pursuing their daily task under the best possible conditions. The Mayor Presents a Clock. • His Worship the Mayor, Alderman Bolton, has pre- sented for use in the magistrates room at the Police Station a gilt clock bearing the following inscription : " Presented to the Magistrates of the County Borough -of Warrington by William Bolton, Esq., Mayor 1904." The clock is a small one of attractive design and it stands on the mantleshelf in a glass case. SUGAR IS DEARER. Golf in the Engadine. The first championship meeting of the recently- instituted Swiss Golf Association has been in progress this week on the Engadine links, near St. Moritz. The chief competition was that for the cup presented by Mr. A. H. Crosfield, of the Royal Liverpool Club. -Mr. J. F. Tarver was the winner. Fins GRATES of every description.—J. S. GAR • LAND, Furnishing Ironmonger, 51, 53, and 55, Bridge-street, Warrineion.--- Advt. 'School Children's Treat. • Mr. W. C. Jones, of Llannercla Park, St. Asaph, and late of the Oaklands, Preston Brook, kindly gave the day• and Sunday 8(410°1 children at Daresbury, Moore, Hatton and Preston Brook a treat on Friday last, in commemoration of the coming of age of his son Mr. Piers Jones. About 350 children assembled in a field kindly placed at, their disposal by Mr. Billington, of the Village Farm, where swing boats, cocoanut stalls, . Aunt Sallies, donkeys, Sm. were provided for their free use until dark. An excellent tea was prepared by teachers and friends from the different schools, and partaken of in the Daresbury Council School at four o'clock, after which sports of all kinds for useful presents, were eagerly indulged in until dusk, when the customary cake and sweets terminated a most • ,enjoyable afternoon. Rumoured Restarting of a St. Helens Glass- works. It 'is rumoured in St. Helens 'that the large and important works of the London and Manchester Plate- Glass Company at Sutton, St. Helens, will shortly be restarted. The works were closed about three years ago, a fact which occasioned considerable distress in the district, as many of the men had been engaged , -there for a great number of years. The company have offered the works for sale and it is understood that an influential deputation of gentlemen have made arrange-, meets to visit them within the next few days. It is understood that the plant has been kept in perfect order, so that if it did happen that the works changed hands they could be started almost immediately. Such an event would be gladly welcomed in the dis- trict, where much distress prevails. SUGAR KEEPS DEAR. Music in the Park. The band of the Ist Volunteer Battalion (P.W.V.) The George Hotel Licence. At a meeting of the General Purposes Committee of the Town Council held on Monday, under the presi- dency of the Mayor (Alderman Bolton) the question of the George Hotel licence was discussed.—The Mayor stated, among other things, that since the meet- ing of the Council, when he said that they' could not go on with Messrs. Walker's offer of £2,500 for the licence without going into a " conspiracy" for certain purposes, circum- stances had changed. There had been changes made in the Licensing Bill during the week that would -make Messrs. Walker's offer (including the taking of • the licence from one district to another) allowable. • Councillor Maginn, in the course of the discussion, stated that he intended to take the necessary steps to have a special meeting of the Council called to deal With the matter in public. It was explained that the letter received from Messrs. Walker and Co. since the meeting of the Council did not imply a withdrawal of ' _their offer to purchase the licence; on the contrary it implied that they were content to leave the whole matter to the judgment and in the hands of the Town Council. ASK for CUNNINGHA.M'S HIGH-CLASS MINERA. WATER S.—Advt, ASK for CITNNLNGHAII'S HIGH-CLASS IiINERAL WATERS.—Advt. Vice-Grand Master Houghton at Croft. Vice-Grand Master, Bro. D. Houghton, D.C.S., of the Warrington equalised district braneh Of the Order of Druids, paid a visit to the Croft United Lodge at the Joiner's Arms on Saturday. A meeting was held m the Lodge room, presided over by the Vice-Grand Master. He was supported by the N.G.A. and V.G.A. and P.N.G.A. of the lodge. The Lodge Secre- tary (Bro. linsworth) showed how steadily the finances of the lodge were increasing.—Bro. Houghton expressed the pleasure it gave him to meet the mem- bers of that lodge, and thanked them for their hearty Welcome. In giving an account of the last delegation, Ifr. Houghton pointed out the changes made in the administration of the Accident Fund, and informed • them that a member of the Warrington district would receive a grant of 15 from this fund on the following Tuesday. This member had, unfortunately, met with an accident whilst working, and it had necessitated the amputation of four fingers on his left hand. The ;pent in no way interfered with the member's benefits trona his lodge or district funds. It WU an addition at a time when a member was in need of the practical B:vrapathy of the members and his society.—The Vice- - Grand Master was heartily thanked for his visit.

In a return showing the assessable value and rates levied since the incorporation of the Borough it appears that the present assessable value to the improvement rate is £224,576 as compared with £220,998 a year pre- vious to That under notice. THE LATEST SOCIETY GAME. First of all a small regiment of scarlet liveried negroes appeared carrying baskets of champagne bottles. These they proceeded to arrange on the smooth green- sward much in the same way as I had seen the posts arranged for driving or bicycle competitions. Next Mrs. S. B. Clewe herself appeared, followed bya pictur- esquely attired coloured woman carrying a basket piled high with brilliant coloured ribbons. Our hostess then, in a few pertinent sentsnces, explained that the entertainment she proposed was to be a 'Living Team." The ladies chosen would be blindfolded and har- nessed in twos or fours with the ribbons. To each team a male driver was appointed. He was to guide his fair steeds in and out of the maze of bottles without their touching or knocking down one. The team who performed this feat in the shortest possible time would receive a prize! Mrs. S.B. Clewe was not a person to do things shabbily in the way of prizes, so the excitement was tremendous. Shouts of laughter more staccato than ever gay frisks and skirmishes of semi-elderly, but wholly juvenile smart women who wanted to be "gees." Screams and directions about "selecting," harnessing, and handling the ribbons. Es- treaties to' spare the whip, and not spoil the child" (said child represented by a fair young thing of fifty- four, in fluttering muslins and gauzy hat!) In fact a scent wholly indescribable, but without doubt most entertaining. At last they were 'marshalled and arranged. Umpires and judges took up their positions, and the first team started to the inspiriting strains of a quick march from the band. Off they trotted with hysterical giggles and prancings and a liberal display of high heels and lace and silken frills. Thecrowd watched them ecstatically. Mrs. S. B. Clewe had achieved another success! How wonderful she was ! How did she ever come to think of such things? It was an absolute novelty ! It was more entertaining even than Mrs. Fitz Gordon's dogs' garden party for poodles only ! So they xhattered and screamed and criticised while I listened and observed— an unconsidered critic of that heedless crowd. Finally there was a roar of excitement. Down went one bottle, swished by a too lavishly adorned skirt. Team No. 11 was disqualified. A second was selected and set forth. It proved worse. Three bottles wore knocked down. The amusement lasted for over an hour. , Not one of the teams achieved the full purpose of the I contest. So they all blamed their drivers, and Mrs. S. B. Clewe gave the prizes (four diamond stars) to the team whose record had been only one bottle. Then there were a series of consolation prizes, after which competitors and audience all sat about on the grass and drank the champagne. It was dusk before the party broke up—all ecstatic over the new pastime, and bent on introducing it, or "running it" witl4 variations, at forthcoming entertainment of their own.—" RITA " in LONDON OPINION. • A GLADSTONIAN REMINISCENCE. Prorogation of Parliament interests few folk save printers, policemen, and cabmen. The session went out very ingloriously, and much in the same fashion as of, old, save that there was an absence of that warm personal touch which Mr. Gladstone invariably imparted The old man, walking-stick in hand.used to stand by the side of the Speaker's chair and make cordial adieux to friend and foe alike as they left the House of Commons —some of them never to return. It was one of those cheerful and good-tempered fancies dear to a septuagenarian's heart. Mr. Balfour would not mind doing it a bit. It is scarcely conceivable that Mr. Chamberlain would take on the job. For Joseph is a man of strong likes and dislikes, and the way in which he has attempted to squelch Winnie during the present year must—to use a Dickensism—have been " nuts " to that forward young gentleman. Remembering how Gladstone was abused by the Irish Nationalists in the olden time—how he was accused of all manner of crimes and misdemeanours, including the wilful punishment of guiltless persons whom he well knew to be innocent, it is not to be wondered at that some of his denouncers were a little shy of coming up to shake hands with the rare old 'un on "breaking up" day.—T. MCDONALD RENDLE IN LONDON OPINION.
